L21: ## 1 Executive Summary
L22: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L23: Midostaurin (proposed proprietary name, RYDAPT) is an orally bioavailable, small-molecule inhibitor of multiple receptor tyrosine kinases, including c-KIT. The review team recommends regular approval of midostaurin for the treatment of adult patients with aggressive systemic mastocytosis (ASM), systemic mastocytosis with associated hematological neoplasm (SM-AHN), or mast cell leukemia (MCL). The proposed starting dose-schedule is 100 mg orally twice-daily, with a 28-day cycle length and continuation until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. Midostaurin is a new molecular entity (NME).
L24: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L25: The submitted data meet the evidentiary standard of effectiveness for adult patients with previously treated or untreated ASM, SM-AHN, and MCL.
L26: The efficacy evaluation is based primarily on overall response rate (ORR) and duration of response (DOR) in a multicenter phase 2 single-arm study (Study D2201) in the intended population. With 6 cycles of midostaurin at the recommended dose-schedule, the ORR, based on modified Valent criteria, was 60% (45% major response, 15% partial response), with an estimated median response duration of 31 months. According to the more stringent IWG-MNRT-ECNM (International Working Group-Myeloproliferative Neoplasms Research and Treatment-European Competence Network on Mastocytosis) consensus criteria, the ORR was 17%, with the median DOR not reached and response durations ranging from 6.8+ to 60.5+ months. According to either set of response criteria, a treatment effect was demonstrated in all histologic subtypes and across other major subgroups. Efficacy outcomes in a supportive single-arm study in the intended population (Study A2213) were comparable. The overall safety profile was acceptable.
L27: Given the rarity of these fatal diseases and the clinically meaningful activity of midostaurin, the review team recommends regular approval.
L28: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L29: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L30: ## Efficacy
L31: The determination of efficacy is based primarily upon ORR and DOR in a multicenter phase 2 study (Study D2201) in the intended population. In the assessment of effectiveness, the review team considered response as determined both by modified Valent or Valent criteria and by the more stringent IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria. In Study D2201, in 89 patients in the primary efficacy population, the ORR with 6 cycles based on modified Valent criteria was 60% (45% MR, 15% PR), with an estimated median DOR of 31 months. In 115 patients evaluated using the more stringent IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria, the ORR with 6 cycles was 17% (2% CR, 15% PR), with response durations ranging from 6.8+ to 60.5+ months. According to either set of response criteria, a treatment effect was demonstrated in all histologic subtypes and across other major subgroups.
L32: The review team recommends that the Prescribing Information present efficacy according to IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria as well as modified Valent or Valent criteria.
L33: ## Safety
L34: Midostaurin carried an acceptable safety profile in the intended population. The primary safety assessment involved an integrated analysis of 142 adult patients with AdSM who received at least one dose of midostaurin at the recommended dose-schedule (100 mg po BID until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity).
L35: The most common ARs (\ge 20\%) were nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, constipation, abdominal pain, edema, fatigue, upper respiratory tract infection, pyrexia, arthralgia, headache, and dyspnea. The most common grade 3-4 hematologic laboratory abnormalities (\ge 20\%) included anemia, thrombocytopenia, and neutropenia.
L36: Eleven percent of patients died on-treatment for reasons unrelated to the underlying malignancy, most often from infection. SAEs were reported in two-thirds of patients, most commonly involving infections and gastrointestinal disorders. AEs led to dose interruption or reduction in 56% of patients, with the leading reasons being nausea, vomiting, QT prolongation, and neutropenia. Treatment discontinuation due to AEs occurred in 21% of patients, most often due to infection, nausea or vomiting, QT prolongation, and hemorrhage.
L37: The supportive safety assessment involved an integrated analysis of 85 other patients with hematologic malignancies patients who received midostaurin at a planned dosage that equaled or approximated the proposed dosage for AdSM. The overall AE profiles in the primary and supportive safety analyses were congruent.
L38: ## Overall benefit-risk assessment for the recommended indication
L39: Advanced SM is rare and fatal. Treatment is generally palliative, there are no approved therapies for SM-AHN or MCL, and imatinib is the only approved therapy for ASM. However, the imatinib indication is applicable to only approximately 10% of patients with ASM, as most cases of SM harbor the D816V c-Kit mutation that confers imatinib resistance.
L40: Based on response rate and response duration, the efficacy results with midostaurin in advanced SM are clinically meaningful, with an overall favorable benefit/risk profile. Given the rarity of this heterogeneous group of diseases and the poor prognosis, the review team recommends regular approval.
L41: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Advanced SM is rare and fatal.
Patients with advanced SM (ASM, SM-AHN, and MCL) have unmet medical needs.

Current Treatment Options	There are no approved therapies for SM-AHN or MCL.
Imatinib is the only approved treatment for ASM. However, the imatinib indication is irrelevant to most patients, because most cases of SM harbor the D816V c-Kit mutation that confers imatinib resistance.
There is an immediate need for effective yet tolerable therapy for patients with all subtypes of advanced SM.

Benefit	Efficacy was evaluated in two multicenter, phase 2 single-arm studies of midostaurin monotherapy in the intended population. In the primary efficacy study (D2201), among 89 patients assessed by the SSC, ORR with 6 cycles based on modified Valent criteria was 60% (45% MR, 15% PR), with an estimated median DOR of 31 months.
According to IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria, among 115 patients assessed in Study D2201, ORR with 6 cycles was 17% (2% CR, 15% PR), with response durations ranging from 6.8+ to 60.5+ months.
A treatment effect was demonstrated in all histologic subtypes.
Efficacy outcomes in the supportive study (A2213) were congruent.
The treatment effect of midostaurin, based on response rate and response duration, is clinically meaningful in patients with advanced SM.

Risk	Safety was evaluated in 142 patients with advanced SM from the above phase 2 trials combined. The median exposure was 11 months.
66% of patients had SAEs, and 21% discontinued treatment due to AEs.
56% of patients had dose modifications for AEs, most commonly due to nausea, vomiting, QT prolongation, and neutropenia.
The most common ARs (\ge 20\%) were nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, constipation, abdominal pain, edema, fatigue, upper respiratory tract infection, pyrexia, arthralgia, headache, and dyspnea.
11% of patients died on-treatment for reasons other than disease progression, most often
The overall risk profile of midostaurin at the proposed dose-schedule is acceptable in the intended population.
The benefit-risk profile of midostaurin is favorable in the intended population.

Risk Management	Serious risks were potentially mitigated in protocols by patient selection criteria and toxicity management guidelines.
Gastrointestinal ARs, which were common, were generally manageable with supportive care and dose modifications.
The majority of dose modifications for toxicity occurred in the first several months, with a median time to first dose modification of 1.6 months.
The PI should describe trial eligibility criteria with respect to end-organ function and performance status.
The PI should recommend prophylactic antiemetics and should contain explicit guidelines for toxicity management.
The PI should recommend particularly close monitoring for actionable toxicities during the first 3 months of treatment. At least weekly monitoring during the first month is recommended.
L43: Appears this way on original
L44: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L45: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L46: SM is a rare, heterogeneous disease caused by the uncontrolled accumulation of neoplastic mast cells, including in the skin, bone marrow, liver, spleen, and gastrointestinal tract. Somatic gain-of-function mutations in *KIT*, particularly the D816V mutation, occur in most adult cases of SM (Garcia-Montero AC et al 2006). The *KIT* D816V mutation, detected in approximately 90% of cases, encodes a constitutively activated receptor tyrosine kinase that drives disease pathogenesis (Garcia-Montero AC et al 2006; Gotlib J et al 2016).
L47: Overviews of the classification and clinical spectrum of SM are presented in the Appendix (Figure 8 and Table 34). Symptoms are caused both by the release of vasoactive mast cell mediators and by organ damage from mast cell infiltration (Gotlib J et al 2016; Pardanani 2015; Tremblay D et al 2015). In aggressive variants, C-findings (clinical manifestations of organ infiltration by mast cells) include cytopenias, skeletal lesions, palpable hepatomegaly with impaired liver function and/or portal hypertension, splenomegaly with hypersplenism, and weight loss due to gastrointestinal involvement (Arber DA et al 2016; Pardanani 2015; Tremblay D et al 2015). Progressive organ infiltration ultimately causes organ failure and death.
L48: The World Health Organization (WHO) subtypes of ASM, SM-AHN, and MCL are regarded as forms of advanced SM (AdSM). In contrast to indolent variants, the prognosis of AdSM is poor, with an estimated median overall survival (OS) of 3.5 years with ASM, 2 years with SM-AHN, and &lt; 6 months with MCL (Lim KH, Tefferi A et al 2009). SM-AHN is itself prognostically heterogeneous, with the prognosis being contingent on the type of AHN (Pardanani et al 2009; Lim KH, Tefferi A et al 2009). The observed risk of leukemic transformation is 5% in ASM, 13% in SM-AHN broadly, and 29% in SM-MDS (Lim KH, Tefferi A et al 2009).
L49: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L50: ## *Available Therapy*
L51: Treatment of AdSM is generally palliative, with limited and largely unsatisfactory options (Lim KH et al 2009).
L52: There are no approved therapies for SM-AHN or MCL. Imatinib mesylate (Gleevec) received regular FDA approval on 10/19/2006 as a single agent for the treatment of adult ASM without the D816V c-Kit mutation or with c-Kit mutational status unknown. The approval was based on 28 patients with ASM, comprising a mixture of literature and study patients, in whom 8 (29%) achieved a complete hematologic response and 9 (32%) a partial hematologic response with imatinib. Response durations ranged from 1+ to 30+ months. However, the imatinib indication
L53: is applicable to only approximately 10% of ASM patients, as SM that harbors a D816V c-Kit mutation is considered resistant to imatinib (Venga-Ruiz A et al 2009).
L54: Cladribine, interferon-\alpha, and hydroxyurea, the most commonly considered cytoreductive agents for AdSM, have been associated with limited major response rates and response durations in mostly small, retrospective studies (Hauswirth AW et al 2004; Lim KH et al 2009). Allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation might be considered, although its value in SM is questionable (Ustun C et al 2014).
L55: ## *Response Assessment*
L56: Response criteria for SM in the literature vary, which limits the generalizability of findings in such a heterogeneous and rare disease.
L57: In the Valent response criteria for AdSM, published in 2003 and supported in a 2007 consensus statement, the status of C-findings is the fundamental basis for response assessment (Valent P et al 2003; Valent P et al 2007). Major response (MR) requires normalization of at least 1 C-finding, with subcategories of MR based on the degree of reduction in mast cell infiltrates, serum tryptase levels, and SM-associated organomegaly (Appendix 13.5, Table 35). Partial response (PR) requires incomplete regression of at least 1 C-finding.
L58: Numerous limitations to the Valent criteria have, however, been recognized (Gotlib J et al 2013) including:
L59: - Responses in C-findings such as ascites and bone lesions are difficult to quantify. This in turn might increase interobserver variability.
- Patients with baseline laboratory values just outside normal range can achieve MR with modest improvement in disease burden.
- Criteria for baseline transfusion dependence and response are not defined.
- The minimal required DOR is unclear.
- Manifestations of organ damage caused by the SM component vs the AHN can overlap.
- Distinguishing cytopenias from bone marrow infiltration vs from other causes (such as hypersplenism, bone marrow fibrosis, and myelosuppression from prior therapy) can prove difficult.
L60: The more rigorous IWG-MRT-ECNM response criteria for AdSM (Gotlib J et al 2013) overcome some (though not all) of these limitations. These criteria are summarized in Appendix 13.6 (Table 38 and Table 39). A comparison of the performance of the Valent and IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria has not, however, been published.
L61: ## 3 Regulatory Background
L62: ## 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L63: Midostaurin is an NME.
L64: ## 3.2. Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
L65: Midostaurin was granted orphan designation for mastocytosis in 4/2010. Based on top-line results from a randomized phase 3 trial (Study A2301), midostaurin was granted Breakthrough Therapy Designation in 1/2016 for treatment of newly diagnosed, FLT3-mutated AML in adults eligible to receive standard induction and consolidation chemotherapy. Rolling review was granted in 4/2016, with a single NDA covering both disease indications. The NDA has been administratively split.
L66: The clinical development program for AdSM was discussed at a Type C end-of-phase 2 meeting in 10/2011 and a Type C meeting in 1/2013. It was agreed that mature data from two single-arm studies (D2201, A2213) with sufficiently durable responses were considered adequate for consideration of regular approval in this rare disease. A Type B pre-NDA meeting for both indications occurred in 9/2015. In a 5/2016 pre-NDA meeting held primarily for the AML indication, the Sponsor agreed to include an integrated summary of safety (ISS) and pooled safety databases from multiple studies.
L67: ## 3.3. Foreign Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L68: Midostaurin has not been marketed outside of the U.S.
L69: Appears this way on original
L70: ## 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L71: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L72: OSI was consulted to conduct inspections for Study D2201 at clinical sites in the Netherlands (Site 180) and Germany (Site 150). These sites were chosen based on relatively high accrual and outliers in efficacy results. Through site inspection, the data were determined to be acceptable.
L73: OSI inspection of the Sponsor, however, identified instances of failure to ensure that Study D2201 was conducted in accordance with the protocol. This mainly consisted of late conduct of monitoring visits for 5 clinical sites. The issues identified do not affect the interpretability of the results.
L74: ## 4.2. Product Quality
L75: No product quality concerns were identified on preliminary CMC review.
L76: ## 4.3. Clinical Microbiology
L77: Not relevant to this application.
L78: ## 4.4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L79: In animal toxicology studies, midostaurin was predominantly associated with toxicities on proliferating tissues, especially the gastrointestinal tract (emesis, diarrhea, intestinal mucosal alteration, gastric hemorrhage), reproductive system (e.g., degenerated spermatogonia), bone marrow (hypocellularity, associated with cytopenias), and lymphoid tissues (depletion, atrophy). Reversible increases in liver enzymes and liver weight occurred in several species. Other findings included the heart (prolongation in P-Q interval, decreased heart rate, myocardial degeneration, myocarditis), lung (bronchopneumonia, lung edema), and possibly the neuromuscular system (ataxia, abnormal gait).
L80: In animal studies, midostaurin caused embryo-fetal toxicity, including fetal anomalies and embryo-fetal death, at doses lower than the recommended human dose. Thus, midostaurin could cause fetal harm when administered to pregnant women. Based on animal data, midostaurin could impair male and female fertility, and could pass into the milk of lactating females.
L81: Midostaurin does not appear to be genotoxic. Carcinogenicity studies were not performed.
L82: ## 4.5. Clinical Pharmacology
L83: ## 4.5.1. Mechanism of Action
L84: Midostaurin is an orally bioavailable, small molecule that potently inhibits a range of receptor tyrosine kinases. In vitro, midostaurin or its two major active metabolites (CGP62221 and CGP52421) inhibit the activity of KIT (wild type and D816V mutant), FLT3, PDGFR, CSF-1R, ROS1, members of the protein kinase C family, and several other receptor tyrosine kinases including FGFR and VEGFR2.
L85: In mastocytosis, somatic gain-of-function point mutations in the *KIT* gene (primarily D816V) lead to constitutive activation and autophosphorylation of KIT, with expansion of clonal mast cell populations. Such activating KIT mutations are detected in most patients with SM. In mast cells, midostaurin inhibits KIT signaling, cell proliferation and histamine release and induce apoptosis in vitro. The in vitro activity against KIT, including KIT mutations associated with resistance to other tyrosine kinase inhibitors such as imatinib (Growney JD et al 2005), provides biologic rationale for the development of midostaurin for SM treatment.
L86: ## 4.5.2. Pharmacodynamics
L87: Cardiac electrophysiology: The Sponsor regarded Study CPKC412A2113A as a thorough QT study. This randomized, placebo- and moxifloxacin-controlled trial evaluated the effect of midostaurin 75 mg BID for 3 days on the QTc interval. No clinically significant prolongation of QTc interval was identified, nor was a significant relationship between changes in QTc and concentrations of midostaurin and its two active metabolites. However, the Interdisciplinary Review Team (IRT) reviewer noted that the study's major limitation is insufficient treatment duration to cover steady state therapeutic exposures of the active metabolite, CGP52421.
L88: Per IRT review, in the AdSM safety population (defined in Section 5.1), 4.7% of patients had a post-baseline QTcF &gt; 480 ms, no patients had a QTcF &gt; 500 ms, and 6.3% had a \DeltaQTcF &gt; 60 ms compared to baseline. In a randomized placebo-controlled study in AML, QTc prolongation was more frequent in patients randomized to midostaurin compared to patients randomized to placebo (QTcF &gt; 480 ms, 10.1% vs 5.7%; QTcF &gt; 500 ms, 6.2% vs 2.6%; and \DeltaQTcF &gt; 60 ms, 18.4% vs 10.7%, respectively).
L89: ## 4.5.3. Pharmacokinetics
L90: Midostaurin is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4.
L91: Midostaurin exhibits time-dependent pharmacokinetics, with an initial increase in trough concentrations (C_{min}) that reach maximal concentrations during the first week followed by a
L92: decline to a steady-state after approximately 28 days. The pharmacokinetics of the metabolite CGP62221 showed a similar trend, whereas plasma concentrations of the metabolite CGP52421 continued to increase after one month of treatment. Notably, the maximal trough and steady-state concentrations of midostaurin, CGP62221, and CGP52421 were similar with 50 mg twice-daily and 100 mg twice-daily with food.
L93: The mean terminal half-life is 21 hours for midostaurin, 32 hours for CGP62221 and 482 hours for CGP52421. Midostaurin is excreted mainly through the feces, primarily as metabolites.
L94: Co-administration of midostaurin with a standard or high-fat meal is associated with 1.2-fold or 1.6-fold increases, respectively, in midostaurin exposure (area under the curve) as compared to administration in a fasted state. Midostaurin maximum concentration is reduced by 20% with a standard meal and by 27% with a high-fat meal, with delayed time to maximal concentration (1 to 3 hours in a fasted state).
L95: Population PK analysis suggests that midostaurin PK is not significantly influenced by sex, age in adults, mild or moderate hepatic impairment, and mild or moderate renal impairment.
L96: ## 4.6. Devices and Companion Diagnostic Issues
L97: A companion device or diagnostic is not included in this application. In 9/2015, FDA determined that a companion diagnostic for KIT D816V mutation is not required, because midostaurin has clinical activity in SM with or without this mutation.
L98: ## 4.7. Consumer Study Reviews
L99: Not applicable.
L100: Appears this way on original

L112: ## 5.2. Review Strategy
L113: The clinical review was conducted by one primary reviewer. Study D2201 served as the primary basis for the review. Data reviewed included the ISS, summary of clinical safety (SCS), summary of clinical efficacy (SCE), individual clinical study reports (CSRs), patient narratives, and numerous information requests (IRs). JMP 11 was used to reproduce key analyses, based on data analysis datasets, and to conduct additional exploratory analyses.
L114: ## 6 Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L115: ## 6.1. Study D2201 (PKC412D2201): A Single Arm, Phase II, Open-Label Study to Determine the Efficacy of 100mg Twice Daily Oral Dosing of Midostaurin Administered to Patients with Aggressive Systemic Mastocytosis or Mast Cell Leukemia +/- an Associated Hematological Clonal Non-Mast Cell Lineage Disease
L116: ## 6.1.1. Study Design
L117: ## Overview and Objectives
L118: This is a single arm, open-label, multicenter phase 2 study of midostaurin 100 mg BID for previously treated or untreated AdSM, with a primary endpoint of ORR by 6 cycles. The study used an adapted Fleming two-stage design followed by an extension phase, with eligibility and response adjudicated by a Study Steering Committee (SSC).
L119: Data submission: all enrolled subjects
L120: Original protocol version: 4/17/2008
L121: Study initiation: 1/6/2009
L122: Status: ongoing; enrollment completed 6/12/2012
L123: Data lock: 12/1/2014
L124: ## Objectives and Endpoints:
L125: Primary: SSC-assessed ORR in the first 6 cycles, defined as PR or MR per modified Valent criteria and revised IWG MDS criteria for transfusion-dependent cytopenias, with confirmation of response required after ≥8 weeks.
L126: ## Secondary:
L127: - DOR, TTR, PFS, OS
- Safety/tolerability
- Histopathologic response (% BM mast cells, serum tryptase levels)
L128: ## Exploratory objectives include:
L129: - Change in all C-findings, including non-measurable C-findings, per protocol response criteria
- PROs (per SF-12, MSAS)
- Pathology and PK correlates
L130: ## **Key Eligibility Criteria (Study D2201)**
L131: - Disease-related:“ASM or MCL +/- AHN” with relapse to 0 to 2 prior regimens of SM treatment
L132: - Relapse/progression after imatinib, if SM has eosinophilia and known F1P1L1-PDGFR\alpha fusion
- At least 1 of the following:\ge 1 measurable C-finding defined as:ANC &lt; 1000/\mu\text{L}, hemoglobin &lt; 10\text{g/dL} or platelets &lt; 100,000/\mu\text{L},
- Hepatomegaly with impaired liver function (+/- ascites, portal hypertension)
- Palpable splenomegaly with hypersplenism
- Malabsorption with hypoalbuminemia and/or significant weight loss
- Transfusion-dependent (TD) anemia (\ge 4 transfused units within 56 days)
- AHN is not life-threatening or acute stage
- &gt; 30 days since chemotherapy – no restriction on steroids
- Age \ge 18 y
- ECOG PS 0-3
- QTcF \le 450 ms
- End-organ function:AST and ALT \le 2.5 \times \text{ULN} unless due to AdSM, then AST/ALT \le 5 \times \text{ULN}
- Bilirubin \le 1.5 \times \text{ULN} unless due to AdSM, then bilirubin \le 3 \times \text{ULN}
- Creatinine \le 2.0 \text{ mg/dL}
- ANC \ge 1500/\mu\text{L}, hemoglobin \le 10 \text{ g/dL}, and platelets \ge 75,000/\mu\text{L} unless disease-related
- No cardiovascular disease, including LVEF &lt; 50\%, recent MI, and poorly controlled hypertension
- No pulmonary infiltrates, even if clinical symptoms of pulmonary infection are resolved
L133: ## Treatment Plan
L134: Midostaurin 100 mg po BID after meals, with 28-day cycle length, continued until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.
L135: Dose modifications for toxicity: Guidelines are summarized in Appendix 13.7 (Table 40).
L136: Supportive care: Inpatient monitoring was required on days 1-3 of cycle 1. A protocol amendment mandated prophylactic antiemetics. Concomitant glucocorticoids were permitted to mitigate symptoms from SM.
L137: Schedule of evaluations: Refer to Appendix 13.8 (Table 41).
L138: ## Statistical Analysis Plan
L139: This was an adaptive Fleming 2-stage design, with an extension phase if the Ho was rejected (Ho: ORR \le 30\%).
L140: ## Analysis sets
L141: - Full analysis set (FAS): all patients assigned to study treatment.
- Primary efficacy population (PEP): response-evaluable patients (based on modified Valent criteria described below) with \ge 1 measurable C-finding or TD anemia. The SSC evaluated efficacy in the PEP.
L142: ## **Response Criteria**
L143: Study D2201 introduced modifications to the Valent criteria as summarized in Table 3. The criteria are compared further in Appendix 13.5 (Table 36, Table 37). Because Valent criteria cannot accurately assess response in patients with transfusion-dependent anemia or thrombocytopenia, the modifications included use of IWG criteria for MDS (Cheson BD et al 2006), which considered transfusion therapy in the assessment of RBC and platelet response.
L144: **Table 3: Comparison of Valent and Modified Valent Criteria**
L145: Criterion	Valent criteria	Modified Valent criteria (D2201)
Minimum increase required for good PR, in addition to C-finding reversion	ANC 100/\muL, hemoglobin 1 g/dL, platelets 10,000/\muL	ANC 200/\muL, hemoglobin 1.5 g/dL, platelets 20,000/\muL
Threshold for minor response	C-finding reversion by > 0% to \le 50\%	C-finding reversion by > 20% to \le 50\%
Non-measurable C-findings (e.g. ascites, bone lesions)	Included	Not included, for stringency of response assessment
Transfusion-dependent anemia	Not differentiated from transfusion-independent anemia as a C-finding	Included as an eligible C-finding
Minimum response duration	Not specified	\ge 8 weeks
L146: Source: Adapted from response to 10/18/2016 IR.
L147: In contrast to Study A2213, Study D2201 considered patients on corticosteroids (&gt; 10 mg prednisone daily or equivalent for \ge 1 cycle) unevaluable for response in the primary analysis.

L164: ## **Review comments:**
L165: - The multiple missing evaluations raise concern about the quality of study oversight and conduct.
- Because patients not meeting disease eligibility criteria were excluded from the PEP and response was adjudicated by the SSC, the overall interpretability of the primary efficacy analysis is not affected.
- However, the large number of other protocol deviations raises potential safety concerns, including in patients whose baseline labs, chest imaging, and cardiac function were either outdated or not performed, and patients whose 28-day safety follow-up was not performed. Such deviations might have modest impact on the overall assessment of safety and efficacy.

L169: ## Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
L170: Approximately 25% of patients had a reported total midostaurin dose of &lt; 100 mg/day (Table 4). To what extent this reflects noncompliance versus toxicity is unclear. The majority (66%) of patients had an RDI of \ge 90%, with a mean RDI of 89% (Table 7).
L171: Per FDA analysis, 22% of patients received at least one dose of corticosteroids during study treatment, most commonly to treat symptoms of SM.

L178: ## 6.2. Study A2213 (PKC412A2213): A Single Arm, Phase II, Open-Label Study To Determine The Efficacy Of Twice Daily Oral Dosing Of PKC412 Administered To Patients With Aggressive Systemic Mastocytosis (ASM) and Mast Cell Leukemia (MCL) +/- Hematological Clonal Non-Mast Cell Lineage Disease
L179: ## 6.2.1. Study Design
L180: ## Overview and Objectives
L181: This is a single-arm, open-label, multicenter phase 2 trial of midostaurin 100 mg po BID for previously treated or untreated AdSM, with a primary endpoint of ORR by 2 cycles per investigator assessment.
L182: Data submission: all enrolled subjects
Original protocol version: 8/18/2004
Study initiation: 6/27/2005
Status: ongoing; enrollment complete
Data lock: 12/3/2012
L183: Clinical Review
Yvette Kasamon, MD
NDA 207997, Orig 1
RYDAPT (Midostaurin)
L184: ## Objectives and Endpoints
L185: Primary: investigator-assessed ORR in the first 2 cycles, defined as PR or MR per original Valent criteria (Appendix, Table 35).
L186: Secondary: include safety and PK, 1-year OS
L187: ## **Key Eligibility Criteria (Study A2213)**
L188: - Disease-related:Histologically documented "ASM or MCL +/- AHN"
- Relapse or progression after imatinib, if SM has eosinophilia and known positivity for F1P1L1-PDGFR\alpha fusion
- &gt; 30 days since chemotherapy; glucocorticoids tapered to off or minimum dose by Day 1
- Age \ge 18 y
- Karnofsky \ge 30% (ECOG PS 0-3)
- End-organ function:Normal AST/ALT, unless due to AdSM, then AST/ALT \le 4 x ULN and/or bilirubin \le 4 x ULN
- Creatinine \le 2.0 mg/dL
- ANC \ge 1500/\muL, hemoglobin \le 10 g/dL, and platelets \ge 75,000/\muL unless disease-related
- No active, unrelated pulmonary disease, HIV infection, or active viral hepatitis
L189: ## **Treatment Plan**
L190: Midostaurin 100 mg po BID after meals for up to twelve 28-day cycles, with option for continued treatment on an extension protocol if there was continued response without requirement for other chemotherapy. Failure to achieve PR or MR after 2 cycles required treatment discontinuation.
L191: **Supportive care:** Inpatient monitoring was required on Days 1-3 of Cycle 1. Prophylactic antiemetics were optional. Concomitant glucocorticoids were permitted to mitigate symptoms from SM.
L192: **Evaluations:** Response assessment occurred after each cycle for Cycles 1-12, with bone marrow biopsy scheduled after Cycles 1, 2, 3, 6, 9 and 12, followed by a reduced evaluation schedule. After treatment discontinuation, patients were followed every 3 months for up to 1 year.
L193: ## **Statistical Analysis Plan**
L194: This was a Simon two-stage design based on response rate (Ho: ORR \le 10%). The same population met criteria for the FAS and PEP.
L195: ## **Response Assessment**
L196: Response was evaluated by the investigator using original Valent criteria (Appendix 13.5).
L197: ## **Review comments:**
L198: - Notably, Study A2213 did not specify that response be maintained for a minimum duration in order to be counted as a response. Unconfirmed ORR is likely an overestimate of the treatment effect, because it includes transient improvements in disease burden.
- In contrast to Study D2201, Study A2213 considered patients on concurrent corticosteroids to be evaluable for response. Because high-dose corticosteroids potentially confound the response assessment, regarding such patients as evaluable might also overestimate the treatment effect.
- For the assessment of efficacy, several other differences between studies D2201 and A2213 are noteworthy and summarized in Table 5.
L199: **Table 5: Efficacy Measures in Study D2201 vs. Study A2213**
L200: Key differences in design	Study D2201	Study A2213
Adjudication of eligibility	Yes	No
Response criteria	Modified Valent and Cheson criteria	Published Valent criteria
C-findings used for response assessment	Measurable C-findings only	Measurable and non-measurable C-findings
Use of transfusion data in response assessment	Yes	No
Confirmation of responses	Yes	No*
Adjudication of responses	Yes, by the SSC	No
Timing of responses for primary efficacy analysis	During first 6 cycles (168 days) of treatment	During first 2 cycles (56 days) of treatment
Duration of participation in study	Until disease progression	Until disease progression; non-responders discontinued after 2 cycles
Duration of OS follow-up	Until end of study (5 years after LPFT, or all patients discontinued)	1 year post treatment

L224: ## 7 Integrated Review of Effectiveness
L225: ## 7.1. Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
L226: The assessment of efficacy is based primarily on Study D2201, with Study A2213 as supportive. As described in Section 6, both studies evaluated a planned midostaurin dose-schedule of 100 mg po BID (cycle length, 28 days) until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity in patients with treated or untreated advanced SM. The studies had similar eligibility criteria, had a primary endpoint of ORR, and had additional outcome measures that included DOR. Notable differences in efficacy measures are discussed in Section 6.2.1.
L227: To integrate the efficacy analysis, the tables that follow present baseline patient and disease characteristics, treatment, and disposition side-by-side for studies D2201 and A2213.
L228: ## **Review comments:**
L229: - The Sponsor presented efficacy for “ASM with or without AHN” and “MCL,” with MCL also including cases of AHN. This classification is unconventional; per the WHO classification, ASM and MCL by definition exclude AHN.
- For evaluations of efficacy to be interpretable and generalizable, analyses must instead be presented according to the established WHO classification of SM subtypes, as determined by central histopathology review. Therefore, FDA required that the Sponsor submit efficacy data according to centrally confirmed histologic subtype.
L230: ## *Baseline Characteristics*
L231: Baseline characteristics of patients in Studies D2201 and A2213 are summarized in Table 6. In both studies, the median age was &gt; 60, and SM-AHN was the common histologic subtype. The majority of patients had a documented D816V mutation at baseline (81% in Study D2201), most had splenomegaly, hepatomegaly, and bone lesions, and approximately half had ascites.
L232: Of the 116 patients treated on Study D2201, an SSC evaluated 89 for response in the primary efficacy analysis because these patients met disease eligibility criteria, including having measurable C-findings. In the PEP, the most common (\ge 20%) measurable C-findings were thrombocytopenia (62%), anemia (31%), transfusion-dependent anemia (22%), hypoalbuminemia (54%), and hyperbilirubinemia (28%)(Source: CSR Table 11-6).

L245: - In Study D2201, although central histopathology review confirmed SM in each case, significant uncertainties about the type of SM remain.
- These limitations in data affect the analysis of efficacy according to SM subtype. The limitations should be mentioned in the PI.
L246: ## **Treatment and Exposure**

Notably, in Study D2201, 59% of patients overall had no prior therapy for SM, 23% had 1 prior therapy, and 17% had 2 or more prior therapies. The distribution in the PEP was similar. Fifteen percent overall had a tyrosine kinase inhibitor (mainly imatinib), with other treatments including cladribine and interferon.

L265: ## 7.1.1. Primary Endpoints
L266: Although both studies evaluated response based on modified Valent or Valent criteria, the reviewer also considered response according to IWG-MRT-ECNM consensus criteria in the evaluation of efficacy.
L267: ## **Review comments:**
L268: - Although “AdSM” is not a WHO category, the term (comprising ASM, SM-AHN, and MCL) is used throughout the literature, including in IWG-MRT-ECNM consensus criteria. Therefore, this review presents efficacy for each SM subtype, as well as for “AdSM” overall.
- Although not prespecified, response evaluation according to IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria is key. The criteria are more rigorous, were designed to address limitations of the Valent criteria, and are expected to be the standard for future studies.
L269: ## **Study D2201 - Response**
L270: ## a. Modified Valent Criteria
L271: The study met its primary efficacy endpoint, rejecting the null hypothesis of an ORR ≤ 30% among the 40 patients enrolled in Stage 1.
L272: Of the 116 patients treated, the SSC evaluated 89 for response according to modified Valent
L273: criteria. The median age in this subgroup was 64 years (range: 25 to 82), 36% had prior therapy for SM, and 82% had the KIT D816V mutation detected at baseline (Table 6). Their median duration of treatment was 11 months (range: &lt; 1 to 68 months), with treatment ongoing in 17%.
L274: Table 9 summarizes response in the PEP according to modified Valent criteria, which required a minimum response duration of 8 weeks. The ORR (major + partial response) in 6 cycles was 60% (95% CI, 49-70) in all AdSM subtypes combined (45% MR), with a median time to response of 9 days. The ORR was 75% in ASM, 58% in SM-AHN, and 50% in MCL, with the lower bounds of the 95% CI ranging from 25 to 48%. Because of the small sample size, the CIs for efficacy measures are wide.

L290: ## **b. IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria**
L291: Per FDA request, the Sponsor also submitted data on response using IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria, which could be applied to 115 of the 116 patients in FAS. Table 10 summarizes response per IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria, which require 12-week confirmation. Midostaurin had activity in all types of AdSM. Notably, however, the observed ORR (defined as CR + PR) and depth of response were lower with these newer criteria. In all patients combined, the ORR was 17% (95% CI, 10-25%), with only 2% CR. Among SM subtypes, ORR ranged from 19 to 31%, with the lower bound of the 95% CI ranging from 5 to 11%.

L300: ## **Review comments:**
L301: - Compared to modified Valent criteria, the response rate is lower according to IWG-
L302: MRT-ECNM criteria, if defined as CR + PR. The depth of response is also less, with most responses being partial. Nevertheless, the responses are durable (minimum response duration &gt; 6 months) and are therefore clinically meaningful.
L303: - The IMG-MRT-ECNM consensus criteria are, in the reviewer's opinion, unclear as to what minimum level of improvement – in particular, at least PR vs at least clinical improvement (CI) – should constitute a formal response for the purposes of a clinical trial. The paper's references to response include CI. However, whether and to what degree CI vs PR vs CR constitute potential measures of clinical benefit in AdSM are undefined.
- (b) (4) Although CI must be maintained for \ge 12 weeks, it requires resolution of only one CI measure (Table 39). The CI criteria vary in stringency. For example, transfusion-dependent anemia becoming transfusion-independent for \ge 12 weeks potentially fulfills criteria for CI, but so does normalization of only 1 liver function test. Thus, while CI might be a surrogate measure of clinical benefit and is reasonably considered "response," the reviewer recommends that only PR and CR be included in the ORR for the purposes of evaluating this application.
L304: ## *Study A2213 - Response*
L305: In Study A2213, 31% of patients had no prior therapy for SM, 38% had 1 prior therapy, and 31% had 2 or more prior therapies. Approximately half had a documented D816V mutation at baseline, and half had received a tyrosine kinase inhibitor (Table 6, Table 7).
L306: ## a. Valent criteria
L307: Table 11 summarizes investigator-assessed response according to Valent criteria. The study met its primary efficacy endpoint, rejecting the null hypothesis of an (unconfirmed) ORR of \le 10\%.
L308: Because response lasting less than 2 months suggests chemoresistant disease, and because Study D2201 required confirmation of response after \ge 8 weeks, Table 11 presents both unconfirmed and confirmed responses.

L314: ## **Review comments:**
L315: - In Study A2213, the CRFs failed to collect C-findings, yet C-findings are the fundamental basis of response assessment. Investigator-based responses were furthermore not formally adjudicated. In response to a 9/20/2016 IR (SDN 14), the Sponsor stated that post-hoc review at each time point in the first 2 months did
L316: not identify inconsistencies in response determination, but that “there was no effort to formally corroborate the best overall response analysis.” No submitted datasets contained structured information on Sponsor-assessed baseline and post-treatment C-findings at each response time point.
L317: - Without such structured data, response cannot be verified, and the contribution of this study to informing efficacy is limited.
L318: ## **b. IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria**
L319: Per FDA request, the Sponsor submitted data on response in Study A2213 by IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria, which could be applied to 23 of 26 patients (Table 12). Of 16 patients with SM-AHN, 2 achieved response (CR or PR) by 2 cycles. Of the 6 patients with MCL, 1 responded. As in Study D2201, the ORR was lower with these criteria as compared to Valent criteria.

L325: ## 7.1.2. Secondary and Other Endpoints
L326: ## *Duration of Response*
L327: In Study D2201, the primary analysis defined DOR as the time from first response to PD, death, or last adequate assessment. In the PEP, DOR per modified Valent criteria, which require that a response be sustained for at least 8 weeks, is shown in Table 11 and Figure 2. With 30-month median follow-up for response duration, the estimated median DOR was 12.7 months (95% CI, 7.4-31.4 months) in patients with SM-AHN and was not reached in patients with ASM or MCL.
L328: Figure 2: DOR (Modified Valent) in D2201 PEP
L329: Source: FDA analysis
L330: Table 10 summarizes DOR based on IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria, which require that a response be sustained for at least 12 weeks. In the FAS, with 35-month median follow-up for response duration, the estimated median DOR was not reached in any subtype. In ASM, SM-AHN, and MCL, the minimum response duration (censored in each case) was 10.2+ months, 6.8+ months, and 8.6+ months, respectively.
L331: ## **Review comment:**
L332: - Summary statistics for response rate and response duration are limited by the small numbers of patients, particularly when using IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria. However, using either set of response criteria, responses to midostaurin are generally durable and thus are clinically meaningful.
L333: DOR in Study A2213 is reviewed in Section 7.1.1.
L334: ## **Time to Response**
L335: For the D2201 PEP, time to confirmed overall response (median 9 days per modified Valent criteria) is shown in Table 9. Figure 3 displays response over time in Cycles 1 through 6. Of note, the responses at each time point are unconfirmed, and thus do not necessarily inform the primary endpoint of confirmed response with 6 cycles. The rates of unconfirmed MR tended to increase during the first several cycles, then stabilize.

L339: ## *Progression-Free Survival and Leukemic Transformation*
L340: PFS is defined as the time from treatment initiation to PD, death, or last adequate assessment, with censoring upon initiation of other treatment in the absence of PD. PFS estimates are shown in Table 13 and Figure 4. As with response rate, CIs are wide due to the small number of patients. Approximately 50% to 60% of the observations were censored.

L346: ## **Review comment:**
L347: - Interpretation of time-to-event endpoints including PFS and OS is challenging in single-arm clinical trials. It is unclear to what extent the outcomes can be attributed to the treatment effect of the drug.
L348: Leukemic transformation was reported in 15 patients (13%) in Study D2201 and no patients in A2213 (Source: ISS Section 7.3.1 and IR). The criteria for PD did not distinguish between leukemic transformation and non-leukemic progression of SM. Of the patients with leukemic transformation, 14 originally had SM-AHN, and 1 had intervening therapy between midostaurin and the diagnosis of acute leukemia.
L349: ## **Review comment:**
L350: - The observed incidence of leukemic transformation is similar to that reported in the literature (Lim KH, Tefferi A et al 2009).
L351: ## **Overall Survival**
L352: FDA defined OS as the time from treatment initiation to death or last patient contact. Table 14 and Figure 5 provide OS estimates in Study D2201, which had an estimated 40-month median follow-up for vital status. Per FDA analysis, the probability of OS in the PEP was 70% (95% CI, 59-79) at 1 year and 51% (95% CI, 39-61) at 2 years.

L358: The Sponsor also compared OS data from studies D2201 and A2213 combined to historical OS data in a registry. The Sponsor concluded that OS, measured from the date of diagnosis, was meaningfully improved in recipients of midostaurin (Source: SCE Section 3.1.13).
L359: ## **Review comment:**
L360: - The analysis of OS relative to historical outcomes is discounted because of its multiple flaws, which include but are not limited to selection bias, the heterogeneity and small number of patients, the single-arm study data, and lack of matching for key patient and disease characteristics.

L371: - the questionable appropriateness of the MSAS, which was developed for solid tumors and combines disease-related and treatment-related symptoms.
- the study sample not being enriched to detect symptom improvement
- the PRO hypothesis not being tested within the main statistical hierarchy (b) (4)
L372: ## **Review comments:**
L373: - (b) (4)
- Because approximately 40% of patients with baseline scores were not evaluable at the post-treatment time point, the degree of improvement in PROs is questionable and subject to overestimation due to selection bias. It would be more conservative to treat the missing data as failures (lack of improvement).
- Interpretation of PROs is challenging in uncontrolled and open-label clinical trials. It is unclear to what extent the outcomes can be attributed to the treatment effect of the drug.
L374: ## 7.1.3. Subpopulations
L375: The small number of patients limits, if not precludes, meaningful subgroup analysis. However, a treatment effect (confirmed overall response) was observed consistently across key subgroups, including patients having prior treatment for SM and patients having a documented D816V c-kit mutation at baseline (Table 16).

L380: ## 7.1.4. Dose and Dose-Response
L381: Data to inform the adequacy of a shorter course of midostaurin or a lower starting dose are not available. The data do not permit an informative analysis of dose-response, since the only efficacy data in AdSM are in patients with a planned midostaurin dose of 100 mg BID, the majority of patients had dose modifications due to toxicity, and the studies are single-arm.
L382: ## **Review comment:**
L383: - The appropriateness of the 100 mg BID starting dose is questionable. By 28 days of midostaurin dosed at 100 mg BID or at 50 mg BID, steady-state levels are quite similar (attributable to autoinduction). Given the similar steady-state levels, the high rate (&gt;50%) of dose modification due to toxicity, and the &gt; 480 hour T1/2 of the active metabolite CPG6525421, the proposed starting dose of 100 mg BID might not be optimal.
L384: ## 7.1.5. Onset, Duration, and Durability of Efficacy Effects
L385: See Sections 7.1.1 and 7.1.2.
L386: ## 7.2. Additional Efficacy Considerations
L387: ## 7.2.1. Considerations on Benefit in the Postmarket Setting
L388: The patients in studies D2201 and A2213 are not necessarily representative of patients with AdSM in the general population, in part given the protocol eligibility requirements. For example, efficacy data in patients with multiply relapsed or progressive AdSM are limited. Efficacy might also be compromised by noncompliance, particularly since the regimen involves a chronic, twice-daily, oral medication that is commonly associated with gastrointestinal ARs.
L389: ## 7.2.2. Other Relevant Benefits
L390: Patients and providers may consider the oral route of administration to be a relevant benefit. The route of administration does not, however, influence this efficacy assessment.
L391: ## 7.3. Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
L392: Based on single-arm clinical trial outcomes, the submitted data meet the evidentiary standard of effectiveness for adult patients with previously treated or untreated ASM, SM-AHN, and MCL. In the assessment of effectiveness, the reviewer considered confirmed response rate and response duration as determined both by modified Valent or Valent criteria and by the more stringent IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria. In Study D2201, in 89 patients in the PEP, the confirmed ORR with 6 cycles, based on modified Valent criteria, was 60% (45% MR, 15% PR), with an estimated median DOR of 31 months. In 115 patients in the FAS, the confirmed ORR with 6 cycles, based on IWG-MRT-ECNM criteria, was 17% (2% CR, 15% PR), with response durations ranging from 6.8+ to 60.5+ months. According to either set of response criteria, a treatment effect was demonstrated in all histologic subtypes and across other major subgroups.
L393: For recommendations on the presentation of efficacy data in labeling, refer to Section 10.1.
L394: ## 8 Review of Safety
L395: ## 8.1. Safety Review Approach
L396: ## *Safety Populations*
L397: The primary safety evaluation consists of an integrated analysis of all 142 patients with AdSM who received at least one dose of midostaurin on Study D2201 or Study A2213. All patients had a planned dose-schedule of 100 mg po BID, dosed continuously until PD or unacceptable toxicity. The safety analysis considered all-causality AEs reported during treatment and up to 28 days after the last midostaurin dose, with the exception of preferred terms (PTs) that directly described the underlying malignancy (e.g. disease progression, mastocytosis, AML).
L398: For supportive safety data, the reviewer pooled 85 patients with other hematologic malignancies treated with midostaurin monotherapy, with a planned dose that equaled or approximated the recommended starting dose for AdSM. These trials, listed in Section 5.1 (Table 2), evaluated a planned dose-schedule of 100 mg BID, its pediatric equivalent (60 mg/m2 BID), or 75 mg TID. Including the latter dose-schedule is justified because of the similar steady-state midostaurin exposures with 75 mg TID and 100 mg BID dosing (Source: SCS Section 1.3.2). Due to significant differences in exposure, safety analyses in AdSM and in other hematologic malignancies are presented separately.
L399: ## *Approach to AE Analysis*
L400: The safety analysis considered all-causality AEs reported during treatment and up to 28 days after the last midostaurin dose. The analysis did not consider reported attributions because the studies were single-arm and midostaurin is an NME. Patients were censored for AE reporting at the time of PD. With the exception of on-treatment deaths, the primary safety analysis is based on originally submitted databases, supplemented by responses to IRs.
L401: With the exception of laboratory values, the Sponsor did not collect baseline grade. Therefore, all non-laboratory events, as well as post-treatment laboratory abnormalities having an unknown baseline grade, are assumed to be treatment-emergent. Notably, the Sponsor also did not collect grade 5 events, such that fatal AEs had a designated maximum grade of 4 and in some cases a grade of 3 (see Section 8.2.3). Accordingly, AEs reported as “grade 3-4” are here termed “grade \ge 3”.
L402: Because laboratory abnormalities were consistently underrepresented in the AE datasets, as compared to the laboratory datasets, the AE tables exclude laboratory terms. Treatment-emergent laboratory abnormalities are instead presented using the dedicated laboratory datasets.
L403: In the main safety analyses, the Sponsor reported AEs using single MedDRA PTs. For increased
L404: sensitivity, FDA instead used a combination of ungrouped and grouped PTs. Except where noted, all analyses presented use the FDA grouping, which is defined in Appendix 13.10 (Table 43).

L424: ## 8.2.3. Adequacy of the Safety Database
L425: The database is adequate to inform safety in the intended population.
L426: ## 8.3. Adequacy of Applicant's Clinical Safety Assessments
L427: ## 8.3.1. Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
L428: No issues arose regarding data integrity. Inadequacies in submission quality were addressed through IRs. The Sponsor responded appropriately to requests for additional information, which included submission of revised and additional datasets.
L429: ## 8.3.2. Categorization of Adverse Events
L430: AEs were graded using NCI CTCAE version 3.0 and were classified using MedDRA terminology. AE analysis datasets contained the full MedDRA hierarchy (LLT, PT, HLT, HLGT, and SOC). The Sponsor performed MedDRA 18.0 harmonization for the purposes of integrated safety analyses. Mapping of verbatim terms ("AEVNAM1A") to MedDRA preferred terms ("PT_TXT") was acceptable, based on review of the AE file (AAEV.xpt) for the AdSM safety pool.
L431: ## 8.3.3. Routine Clinical Tests
L432: In studies D2201 and A2213, the schedule of routine clinical evaluations was adequate for safety analysis. Refer to Appendix 13.8 for the schedule of Study D2201 assessments. This monitoring schedule in the protocol included testing on day 3 and weekly in cycle 1, every other week in cycle 2, then after each cycle.
L433: ## 8.4. Safety Results
L434: ## 8.4.1. Deaths
L435: In the AdSM safety population, on-treatment deaths unrelated to the underlying malignancy occurred in 16 patients (11%), most commonly from infection (sepsis or pneumonia), followed by cardiac events (Table 19). Based on review of the death narratives, FDA determined and in some cases revised the cause of death based on available data. Of the on-treatment deaths attributable to disease progression, 4 were also attributable to infection (Table 19). No on-treatment deaths were reported in the other pool of hematological malignancies patients treated with midostaurin monotherapy (planned total daily dose, 200-225 mg).

L444: - A notable limitation of the safety databases is the failure to capture grade 5 events, which instead had a maximum designated grade of 4. With the exception of one study (A2301), the Sponsor did not capture or present grade 5 AEs in any of their studies included in the ISS. In response to an IR, the Sponsor stated that the AdSM AE datasets included all reported, on-treatment fatal events, but that the events had a designated grade of &lt; 5.

L453: ## 8.4.3. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects
L454: Because of the significantly longer exposure in the AdSM safety population compared to the other hematological malignancies safety population, the analysis of treatment discontinuation or modification is restricted to AdSM.

L484: ## **Review comments:**
L485: - Because the majority of dose modifications or first dose modifications occurred in the first 3 months, close monitoring for actionable toxicities is recommended particularly during this time period.
L486: - Although a QT study did not identify a significant impact of midostaurin on QTc, QT prolongation was a commonly reported AE (&gt;10%), and was a leading reason for treatment modification and discontinuation. The question arises as to whether concomitant QT-prolonging medications (potentially including antiemetics and antifungal azoles) contributed to the QT prolongation. However, the submitted data do not permit an analysis of this potential interaction. EKG monitoring should be considered in patients on concurrent medications with QT-prolonging potential.
- The appropriateness of the dose modification guidelines for toxicity is questionable, as they do not appear to be supported by the pharmacokinetic data.

L502: **Infection:** For the AdSM safety population, FDA requested data on ANCs associated with the Sponsor summarized the ANCs associated with all grade \ge 3 AEs captured under the “infections and infestations” SOC (Source: response to 12/14/2016 IR). Most of these infections occurred in the absence of documented grade 4 neutropenia.
L503: ## **Review comment:**
L504: - Because the AdSM studies were single-arm, one cannot discern an increased infection risk from midostaurin from the overall increased infection risk seen in immunosuppressed patients. Most infections occurred without grade 4 neutropenia. In Study A2301, among patients randomized to the midostaurin arm (planned dose-schedule, 50 mg BID), increased risks of infection (apart from device-related infections), grade \ge 3 neutropenia, and grade \ge 3 lymphopenia were not observed as compared to patients randomized to the placebo arm. Animal data did not suggest an excess infection risk with midostaurin, based on Pharmacology/Toxicology review.
L505: ## **Cardiac dysfunction:**
L506: Reports of cardiac dysfunction in Study D2201 are summarized in Appendix 13.11 (Table 44). In Study D2201, echo or MUGA was required at baseline, after
L507: Cycles 3, 6 and 12, then yearly and at end of treatment. As summarized in Appendix 13.11 and Section 8.4.1, two deaths occurred from CHF: one from severe valvular disease, and one from right-sided CHF in setting of COPD and a preserved LVEF. The Sponsor described 3 other cases of notably reduced LVEF in patients with a normal baseline (Table 44). Limitations of this analysis, however, include a) the lack of central review of echocardiograms, and b) the amount of missing data.
L508: In Study A2301, no differences in the risk of cardiac dysfunction were identified in the midostaurin arm as compared to the placebo arm. However, the study was not specifically designed to evaluate changes in cardiac function.
L509: ## **Review comment:**
L510: - Based on the Pharmacology/Toxicology review, cardiac dysfunction from midostaurin is plausible.
L511: (b) (4)
L512: **Pulmonary toxicity:** The Sponsor proposed a Warning and Precaution of pulmonary toxicity / interstitial lung disease. Per FDA request, the Sponsor submitted additional data on pulmonary complications in the ISS pool (response to 12/29/2016 IR, SDN 61). Based on a MedDRA SMQ of interstitial lung disease, the Sponsor reported 5 cases of pulmonary toxicity, each grade &lt;3, in patients with AdSM. However, as summarized in Appendix 13.11 (Table 45), at least two of these cases were from infection. For a description of additional cases of pulmonary toxicity, please refer to the Clinical Review of the AML indication (NDA 207997 Original 2, Ward and Przepiorka 2017). Based on the narratives submitted for the ISS pool, the majority of cases were of pulmonary infection rather than pulmonary toxicity.
L513: ## **Review comment:**
L514: - In the ISS pool, multiple cases categorized as pulmonary toxicity were instead infectious. However, midostaurin might have contributed to some cases of interstitial lung disease or pneumonitis, and studies D2201 and A2213 excluded patients with pulmonary infiltrates in order to mitigate the risk. Pulmonary toxicity from midostaurin is possible, based on Pharmacology/Toxicology review. Although clinical data are limited, this reviewer supports the Warning and Precaution on pulmonary toxicity.
L515: **Other AESIs:** The Sponsor also evaluated hematologic toxicity (anemia, leukopenia, and thrombocytopenia) using grouped AE terms. However, the reviewer found that incidences of hematologic toxicities were underrepresented in this analysis, as compared to the analysis of treatment-emergent hematologic abnormalities using the laboratory datasets (Table 32).

L578: ## 8.5. Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
L579: Submission-specific safety issues are covered in Sections 8.4.1 through 8.4.6, including adverse events of special interest (Section 8.4.4) and the time course of common AEs (Section 8.4.5).
L580: ## 8.6. Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
L581: In patients with AdSM, the Sponsor reported that the median RDI and median duration of midostaurin exposure were similar in those aged &lt; 65 years (N= 78) and those aged ≥ 65 years (N = 64) (Source: ISS Section 2.1.2). SAEs and grade 3-4 AEs were reported more frequently in patients aged ≥ 65 years, whereas the overall incidence of AEs was generally similar (Source: SCS Section 2.5.1).
L582: Overall, reporting of AEs was balanced between genders (Source: ISS Section 4.5.2.1). Because most of the patients were Caucasian, no meaningful conclusions can be drawn from subgroup analysis by race.
L583: ## 8.7. Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
L584: Section 4.5.2 describes a dedicated QT study (A2213). For results of a dedicated study in patients with no, mild, or moderate hepatic impairment (A2116), please refer to the Clinical Pharmacology review. A dedicated renal impairment study was not conducted for midostaurin.
L585: ## 8.8. Additional Safety Explorations
L586: ## 8.8.1. Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
L587: See Section 4.4.
L588: ## 8.8.2. Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
L589: See Section 4.4. There are no human data on fertility or fetal effects.
L590: ## 8.8.3. Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L591: Not applicable for the proposed indication.
L592: ## 8.8.4. Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
L593: Doses above or below 100 mg BID (apart from dose reductions for toxicity) have not been examined in patients with AdSM. No studies have been conducted that assess overdose, withdrawal, and rebound effects of midostaurin.
L594: ## 8.8.5. Safety in Patients with Hepatic Impairment
L595: Given the few patients with AdSM having moderate (n = 8) or severe (n = 2) hepatic impairment at baseline (Table 18), no meaningful conclusions regarding safety can be made for these subgroups. On FDA analysis, of the 33 patients with at least mild hepatic impairment at
L596: baseline, 25 (76%) had SAEs, excluding SAEs related directly to the underlying malignancy. Grade 3-4 AEs in patients with baseline hepatic impairment are summarized in Table 33.

L602: ## **Review comment:**
L603: - Hepatic impairment is a common manifestation of ASM and is a potential C-finding. The pharmacokinetic profile, safety and tolerability of midostaurin in patients with severe hepatic dysfunction are unknown.
L604: ## 8.8.6. Day 120 Safety Update
L605: The safety update, submitted 12/23/2016 with a database lock of 7/1/2016 and Novartis safety database listings from 7/2/2016 to 10/30/2016, mainly consisted of updated Study D2201 data (300 patient-months) and additional follow-up on the 7 patients on Study A2213 whose treatment was previously ongoing. The updated information did not reveal new safety concerns. Information on new deaths or updated causes of deaths is incorporated into Section 8.4.1.
L606: ## 8.9. Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L607: ## 8.9.1. Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L608: Not applicable, as midostaurin is not marketed in any country.
L609: ## 8.9.2. Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L610: Because recipients of marketed drug might not have met eligibility criteria for the studies supporting marketing approval, AEs may occur with higher incidence and severity in the postmarket setting. For example,
L611: - Safety in patients with significant comorbidities has not been defined, including in the setting of cardiac dysfunction, pulmonary dysfunction or infiltrates, and moderate and severe hepatic impairment.
- Safety in patients with multiply relapsed or progressive AdSM is not well defined.
L612: New safety signals might also emerge with larger numbers of patients exposed to midostaurin.
L613: ## 8.10. Additional Safety Issues From Other Disciplines
L614: Strong CYP3A Modulators: Strong CYP3A inhibitors might adversely affect the tolerability of midostaurin. The safety and tolerability of midostaurin in patients taking strong CYP3A4 inhibitors are, however, incompletely characterized. Per the Clinical Pharmacology review, caution and consideration of alternative agents are recommended, as concomitant use of strong CYP3A4 inhibitors may increase exposure to midostaurin. In the presence of strong CYP3A inhibitor, midostaurin exposure was found to increase by up to 10-fold following a single midostaurin dose and by approximately 2-fold around steady state.
L615: ## 8.11. Integrated Assessment of Safety
L616: Midostaurin carries an acceptable safety profile in the intended population. The primary safety assessment involved an integrated analysis of 142 adult patients with AdSM who received at least one dose of midostaurin on Studies D2201 or A2213 (planned regimen, 100 mg po BID until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity). The median exposure to midostaurin was 11 months.
L617: The most common ARs (\ge 20\%) were nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, constipation, abdominal pain, edema, fatigue, upper respiratory tract infection, pyrexia, arthralgia, headache, and dyspnea. The most common grade 3-4 hematologic laboratory abnormalities (\ge 20\%) included anemia, thrombocytopenia, and neutropenia.
L618: Eleven percent of patients died on-treatment for reasons unrelated to the underlying malignancy, most often from infection (sepsis or pneumonia), followed by cardiac events. SAEs were reported in two-thirds of patients, most commonly involving infections and
L619: gastrointestinal disorders. AEs led to dose interruption or reduction in 56% of patients, usually in the first 5 months. The leading reasons for dose modifications (\ge 10\%) were nausea, vomiting, QT prolongation, and neutropenia. Treatment discontinuation due to AEs occurred in 21% of patients, most often due to infection, nausea or vomiting, QT prolongation, and hemorrhage.
L620: Supportive safety data were obtained from 85 patients with other hematological malignancies who received midostaurin monotherapy at a planned dosage that equaled or approximated that in AdSM (median exposure, 1 month). The overall AE profile was congruent with that described in the primary safety analysis.
L621: ## 9 Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultation
L622: Midostaurin was not presented to the Oncologic Drug Advisory Committee or other external consultants.
L623: ## 10 Labeling Recommendations
L624: ## 10.1. Prescribing Information
L625: The following are recommendations for the RYDAPT PI based on this review:
L626: **Indication:** Regular approval of midostaurin for the treatment of adult patients with aggressive systemic mastocytosis, systemic mastocytosis with associated hematological neoplasm, or mast cell leukemia (100 mg po BID with food, until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity)
L627: ## **Dosing and administration:**
L628: - Recommend prophylactic antiemetics.
- (b) (4) toxicity management guidelines to match protocol D2201.
- Recommend particularly close monitoring for actionable toxicities, such as gastrointestinal toxicities, during the first 3 months of treatment. The minimum suggested monitoring schedule is once weekly during cycle 1, every other week during cycles 2 and 3, and monthly thereafter.
- Recommend EKG monitoring in patients taking concomitant medications that can prolong the QT interval.
L629: ## **Efficacy:**
L630: - Present efficacy according to IWG-MRT-ECNM response criteria, in addition to modified Valent or Valent criteria, with response defined as \ge PR.
L631: - Present \ge 8 week confirmed, [REDACTED] (b) (4) response rates for Study A2213.
- Present efficacy outcomes according to the WHO classification of SM, [REDACTED] (b) (4)
- [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L632: ## Safety:
L633: - Include pulmonary toxicity as a Warning and Precaution.
- Present ARs using selected grouped preferred terms, [REDACTED] (b) (4) for more sensitive and informative labeling.
- In the table of common ARs in the AdSM safety population,Add the following preferred terms or grouped preferred terms: gastrointestinal hemorrhage, rash, QT prolongation, musculoskeletal pain, arthralgia, abdominal pain, and insomnia.
- Categorize [REDACTED] (b) (4) ARs as “grade 3 to 5,” [REDACTED] (b) (4)
- Add myocardial infarction, renal failure, enteritis, pneumonitis or interstitial lung disease, and respiratory failure as clinically significant, uncommon ARs.
- Include all of the most common laboratory abnormalities and all grades in Section 6, [REDACTED] (b) (4)
- Present treatment-emergent laboratory abnormalities [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L634: ## 10.2. Patient Labeling
L635: Review of patient labeling by the Division of Medical Policy Programs was ongoing at the time of this review.
L636: ## 11 Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
L637: This reviewer and the Division of Risk Management reviewer do not recommend a REMS. Based on the risk/benefit profile of midostaurin, safety issues can be adequately managed through appropriate labeling and routine post-marketing surveillance.
L638: ## 12 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L639: For the AdSM indication, no postmarketing requirements or postmarketing commitments are recommended from a clinical standpoint. The reviewer considered a PMC to evaluate a lower starting dose of midostaurin for AdSM. However, given the rarity and prognostic heterogeneity
L640: of AdSM, as well as the anticipated approval of the midostaurin 100 mg BID dose-schedule, an informative trial would be challenging to conduct. In order to mitigate serious toxicities, the PI will include instructions for frequent monitoring. The PI will also include more detailed guidelines on toxicity management(b) (4)

L674: ## 13.5. Valent and Modified Valent Response Criteria
L675: **Table 35: Original Valent Criteria**
L676: Copyright Material Withheld
L677: Source: Gotlib J et al 2013 synopsis of Valent P et al 2003
L678: Table 36: Response Definitions in Studies D2201 and A2213
L679: D2201:
Modified
Valent criteria	Response category	Definition
Major response (MR)	Complete resolution of at least one C-finding, and no confirmed progression in other C-findings.
Complete remission (CR)	Disappearance of all mast cell infiltrates in affected organs, decrease of serum tryptase level to <20 ng/mL, and disappearance of SM-associated organomegaly.
Incomplete remission (IR)	Decrease in mast cell infiltrates in affected organs and/or substantial decrease (>50%) of serum tryptase level from baseline and/or visible regression of organomegaly
Pure clinical response (PCR)	No significant response in mast cell infiltrates, tryptase levels, organomegaly that could reach the score of CR or IR.
Partial response (PR)	Measurable improvement in one or more C-finding(s) without confirmed progression in other C-findings.
Good partial response (GPR)*	>50% regression of one or more C-findings and no progression in other C-findings.
Minor response (MinR)*	>20% to ≤ 50% regression of one or more C-findings and no progression in other C-findings.
No response (NR)	
Stable disease (SD)	C-findings show constant range, not meeting the criteria for a response or progressive disease
Progressive disease (PD)	At least one C-finding show progression, defined as >20% worsening compared to the last value before the start of study drug
L680: * GPR and MinR are not defined for C-findings of TD anemia and TD thrombocytopenia.
L681: A2213:
Original
Valent criteria	Response category	Definition
Major response (MR)	Complete resolution of at least one C-finding and no progression of other C-findings.
Complete remission (CR)	Complete disappearance of mast cell infiltrates in affected organs, decrease serum tryptase levels to <20 ng/mL, and disappearance of SM-associated organomegaly
Incomplete remission (IR)	>50% decrease in mast cell infiltrates in affected organs and/or >50% decrease of serum tryptase level, and/or >50% visible regression of organomegaly.
Pure clinical response (PCR)	Without decrease in mast cell infiltrates, without decrease in tryptase levels, and without regression of organomegaly.
Partial response (PR)	Incomplete regression of one or more C-findings without complete regression and without progression in other C-findings
Good partial response (GPR)	>50% regression of one or more C-findings and no progression in other C-findings
Minor response (MinR)	≤ 50% regression of one or more C-findings and no progression in other C-findings.
No response (NR)	C-findings persistent or progressive*
Stable disease (SD)	C-findings show constant range
Progressive disease (PD)	At least one C-finding show progression
L682: *In the case of progressive C-findings and a documented response in other C-findings, the summary response designation was progressive disease
L683: Source: SCE Table 3-3 (Study D2201) and Table 3-4 (Study A2213)

(Proposed) Trade Name	Rydapt®
Applicant	Novartis
Formulation(s)	Soft capsule 25 mg
Dosing Regimen	50 mg twice daily on days 8 to 21 of each cycle of induction and consolidation chemotherapy
Applicant Proposed Indication(s)/Population(s)	Rydapt is indicated in combination with standard induction and consolidation chemotherapy (b) (4) for adult patients with newly diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia (AML) who are FLT3 mutation-positive, as detected by an FDA approved test.
Recommendation on Regulatory Action	Full approval for revised indication
Recommended Indication(s)/Population(s) (if applicable)	Rydapt is indicated, in combination with standard cytarabine and daunorubicin induction and cytarabine consolidation chemotherapy, for the treatment of adult patients with newly diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia (AML) who are FLT3 mutation-positive, as detected by an FDA approved test.
