L1223: 1 For EFS analysis, patients who did not achieve a CR by day 60 were assigned an event date of day +1
L1224: The FDA has concerns regarding whether the presence of a FLT3 mutation is the best predictor of clinical benefit from midostaurin, since midostaurin inhibits multiple kinases that have been implicated in AML pathogenesis, and the applicant has collected only minimal data in FLT3 wild type AML in support of their development in a diagnostically selected patient population.
L1225: In response (dated November 30, 2016, SDN 44) to an FDA information request, the applicant
L1226: submitted results of their analysis of NPM1 mutations in patients on Study A2301. In this response, the applicant showed that NPM1 mutations were a positive predictor of overall survival (consistent with the literature), and that the treatment effect of midostaurin on OS was present in both NPM1 wild type (HR 0.74, p=0.038) and NPM1 mutated patients (HR 0.72, p=0.029). However, the treatment effect of midostaurin on EFS was only observed in patients with mutant NPM1 (HR 0.66, p=0.001), with little to no effect on EFS in patients with wild type NPM1 (HR 0.92, p=0.282). Furthermore, midostaurin improved CR rates in patients with mutant NPM1 (60% vs 40%, p=0.010), but not in patients with wild type NPM1 (46% vs 48%, p=0.589). This discrepancy between efficacy variables raised the concern that the overall survival benefit observed in patients with wild type NPM1 was due to factors other than the study treatment.
L1227: Additional information was requested from the applicant. In their response (dated January 25, 2016, SDN 70), additional sensitivity analyses performed by the applicant, which were confirmed by the FDA, showed that if the standard definition of CR is used (CR after 1 or 2 cycles of induction) rather than the protocol defined CR within 60 days, midostaurin improves CR rates in wild type NPM1 patients (57% vs 51%) and also improved EFS in wild type NPM1 patients (HR 0.72, 95% CI: 0.53, 0.96), with effect size now more similar to that in NPM1 mutant patients. Taken together, this data suggest that while NPM1 mutations are a positive prognostic indicator in AML, they do not appear to be a predictor of clinical benefit to midostaurin, and that midostaurin provides clinical benefit to AML patients with wild type and mutant NPM1.
L1228: The frequency of NPM1 mutations in women on Study A2301 was approximately 59% on the midostaurin arm and 66% on the placebo arm, while the frequency of NPM1 mutations in men was approximately 50% on both arms. This increased frequency of NPM1 mutations in women, and particularly in women on the placebo arm, combined with the disproportionate number of women randomized to the placebo arm, provides a plausible biologic explanation for the unexpectedly high survival of women on the placebo arm of the trial. These findings suggest that women are likely to benefit from the addition of midostaurin to standard chemotherapy in the treatment of their AML, although further studies to elucidate the magnitude of this benefit in patients with NPM1 mutations would be useful for defining the most appropriate patient population to receive midostaurin.
L1229: ## 6.2. Study A2106
L1230: ## 6.2.1. Study Design
L1231: ## Overview
L1232: Study A2106 was a phase I, open-label study to determine the safety, tolerability and pharmacokinetics of several different doses and schedules of midostaurin administered in combination with standard chemotherapy in patients with newly diagnosed AML. Evaluation of efficacy was a secondary objective. The study was conducted at a total of six sites in the United
L1233: ## Clinical Review
L1234: Ashley Ward, MD
L1235: NDA 207997
L1236: Rydapt® (midostaurin)
L1237: States and Germany between April 2003 and June 2011. The last treatment for the last patient was administered in September 2008.
L1238: ## Trial Design
L1239: Patients age \ge 18 and \le 60 years with newly diagnosed AML (FLT3 mutation allowed but not required) were alternately assigned to one of two treatment arms. All patients received induction with daunorubicin 60 mg/m2/day IV over 30 minutes on each of days 1-3 and cytarabine 200 mg/m2/day over 24 hours for 7 days continuous infusion. If a second cycle of induction therapy was required due to inadequate response, daunorubicin and cytarabine were administered on days 1-2 and days 1-5, respectively. Patients who obtained a CR after 1 or 2 cycles of induction received up to 3 cycles of consolidation therapy with cytarabine 3 g/m2 IV over 3 hours every 12 hours on days 1, 3 and 5 (total of 6 doses).
L1240: When the study started, midostaurin was administered during all cycles of induction and consolidation as follows:
L1241: - Arm 1: Sequential administration (midostaurin 100 mg bid days 8-28)
- Arm 2: Concomitant administration (midostaurin 100 mg bid days 1-28)
L1242: After patients completed consolidation, midostaurin was administered as single-agent maintenance therapy on the same schedule until relapse, unacceptable toxicity, or a maximum of 3 years. As the study progressed, the protocol was amended several times to adjust the treatment regimen, first to reduce the duration of midostaurin dosing (to days 8-21 on Arm 1 and to days 1-7 and 15-21 on Arm 2), then to reduce the midostaurin dose (from 100 mg bid to 50 mg bid on both arms).
L1243: Patients were continuously monitored for adverse events and had blood counts at least 3x weekly and serum chemistries at least weekly during induction and consolidation cycles. An echocardiogram was obtained at baseline and at the end of study. Response assessments were performed after every treatment cycle.
L1244: ## 6.2.2. Study Results
L1245: ## Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L1246: The applicant provided attestation that this study was conducted in accordance with U.S. regulations governing the protection of human subjects, institutional review boards, and the obligations of clinical investigators in accordance with good clinical practice (GCP).
L1247: ## Financial Disclosure
L1248: A summary of financial disclosures for Study A2106 is provided in the appendix (Section 13.2). The applicant was unable to obtain financial disclosure information from 7 of the 40
L1249: ## Clinical Review
L1250: Ashley Ward, MD
L1251: NDA 207997
L1252: Rydapt® (midostaurin)
L1253: investigators on Study A2106. All were sub-investigators at a single study site, which enrolled (b)(6)% of the subjects on the clinical trial. No financial interests or arrangements were reported in the other investigators.
L1254: ## Patient Disposition
L1255: The study screened 73 patients and enrolled 69 patients (34 in Arm 1 and 25 in Arm 2). The applicant's summary of patient disposition on Study A2016 is reproduced in Table 13. Study completion rate was highest (65%) in patients who received midostaurin 50 mg bid on days 8-21 of each cycle of therapy. Further detail about the reasons for treatment discontinuation was not provided in the applicant's submission, with the exception of adverse events that prompted treatment discontinuation, which are included in the discussion of safety below.

L1260: ## Demographic Characteristics
L1261: Study A106 enrolled equal numbers of men (n=35, 51%) and women (n=34, 49%). Most of the study subjects were white (n=58, 84%). The median age of study participants was 49 (range 20-65). One 65-year old subject was treated on the study which constituted a major protocol violation as the eligibility criteria required age ≤ 60.
L1262: ## Clinical Review
L1263: Ashley Ward, MD
L1264: NDA 207997
L1265: Rydapt® (midostaurin)
L1266: ## Other Baseline Characteristics
L1267: According to information recorded in the CRFs, 19 patients (28%) had FLT3 mutations at baseline. Of these, 13 had a FLT3-ITD and 6 had FLT3-TKD. The results for the central FLT3 mutation status assessment were consistent with that reported in the CRF for the majority of patients. A divergent baseline result was reported for 2 patients, both of whom had FLT3 mutations according to the investigator, but were FLT3 wild type according to the central laboratory. For an additional 2 patients assessed as FLT3 wild type by the investigator, FLT3 status could not be confirmed by central testing (central result: unknown).
L1268: ## Efficacy Results - Primary Endpoint
L1269: The primary efficacy variable in this study was CR, defined as an ANC \ge 1500/\muL (with no G-CSF for at least 5 days), platelet count \ge 100 x 109/L, no leukemic blasts in the peripheral blood and transfusion independence, as well as a bone marrow with adequate (&gt;20%) cellularity that showed &lt; 5% blast cells and no extra-medullary leukemia. The applicant's analysis included all CRs that occurred within 42 days of treatment discontinuation, which included 5 subjects that obtained a CR during consolidation therapy. However, for consistency with the analysis of Study A2301, the FDA analyzed only those CRs that occurred after cycle 1 or 2. By this definition, a total of 34 patients (49%) achieved a CR. The CR rate was highest in patients who received midostaurin at 50 mg bid (Table 14).
L1270: **Table 14: Study A2106 – Complete Responses**
L1271: Midostaurin 100 mg bid initial schedule	Midostaurin 100 mg bid reduced frequency	Midostaurin 50 mg bid	Total

L1272: Source: FDA analysis
L1273: 1 Only CRs obtained after 1 or 2 cycles of induction were included
L1274: Historical rates of CR after standard induction chemotherapy (without midostaurin) in patients with unselected, previously untreated AML are 60-70% in patients age 40-60 and approximately 50% in patients older than 60 (Mayer et al, NEJM 1994). The lower CR rates observed on this study, which enrolled only patients younger than 60, may reflect small sample size and high study discontinuation rates, particularly in patients receiving 100 mg bid of midostaurin. Prior studies have generally found no difference in CR rates between wild-type and mutant FLT3 AML (Levis and Small, Leukemia 2003).
L1275: Reviewer comment: The results of Study A2106 support the selection of the 50 mg bid dose of midostaurin, administered after chemotherapy (sequentially) in each cycle of standard induction with daunorubicin and cytarabine and consolidation with high dose cytarabine, for pivotal Study A2301.
L1276: By FDA analysis of the datasets provided by the applicant, CR rate was higher in patients with a baseline FLT3 mutation by central assessment (14 out of 19, 74%) than in those with wild type FLT3 (19 out of 50, 40%). The 2 patients assessed as FLT3 wild type by the investigator without central confirmation were included in the FLT3 wild type subset for the purposes of this analysis.
L1277: Reviewer comment: While the numbers are small and the analysis post-hoc, the observation that CR rates after 1 or 2 cycles of induction therapy were higher for patients with baseline FLT3 mutations compared to patients with wild type FLT3 makes the applicant's decision to study midostaurin further only in FLT3 mutated patients understandable. However, it is important to note that while CR rates after induction have been thought to predict long-term survival in AML, several recent studies of new drugs in AML have reported increased CR rates compared with controls but no corresponding increase in survival (Estey et al, Leukemia 2016), raising concerns about the utility of CR as a predictive biomarker.
L1278: ## 6.3. Study ADE02T
L1279: ## 6.3.1. Study Design
L1280: ## Overview
L1281: Study ADE02T was an investigator-sponsored, phase II, single-arm, open-label, multi-center study designed to evaluate the safety and efficacy of midostaurin administered in combination with standard chemotherapy in patients with newly diagnosed, FLT3-ITD, positive AML. The primary endpoint was EFS, with secondary efficacy endpoints including CR rate, relapse-free survival, and overall survival. Of note, the trial was not randomized and the intent was to compare the results observed on the study with historical controls. The interim clinical study report (CSR) states that the historical control population consisted of 588 intensively treated patients with FLT3-ITD and a concurrent NPM1 mutation, which had a 70% CR rate and 25% 2-year EFS, although a reference was not cited. Evaluation of efficacy included pre-defined (by protocol amendment) subgroup analyses in age groups 18-60 years and 61-70 years. The study was initiated in June 2012 and is ongoing at 49 sites in Germany and 5 sites in Austria. For the interim report provided in the submission, a summary of data received up to the data cut-off date of December 31, 2015 was reported.
L1282: Reviewer comment: Time-to-event variables cannot be evaluated on a single arm trial. Furthermore, it is not clear that the stated historical control population, which consisted of
L1283: ## Clinical Review
L1284: Ashley Ward, MD
L1285: NDA 207997
L1286: Rydapt® (midostaurin)
L1287: patients with *NPM1* mutations (known positive prognostic indicator), is relevant for this study.
L1288: ## Trial Design
L1289: The trial schema for Study ADE02T is provided in Figure 6. Patients age 18 to 70 with newly diagnosed AML and FLT3-ITD as assessed by a central laboratory were eligible for the study, except for patients with t(8;21)(q22;q22), inv(16)(p13.1q22), t(16;16)(p13.1;q22), *PML-RARA* or any other variant translocations with other *RARA* gene fusions. Patients were required to have a WHO performance status of \le 2.
L1290: Figure 6: Study ADE02T – Trial Schema
L1291: Source: ADE02T Interim Clinical Study Report Table 10-1 dated April 30, 2016
L1292: All patients received at least one dose of induction with daunorubicin 60 mg/m2/day IV over 30 minutes on each of days 1-3 and cytarabine 200 mg/m2/day over 24 hours for 7 days continuous infusion. Patients in a CR after 1 or 2 cycles of induction went on to consolidation therapy. Where possible, patients underwent allogeneic HSCT, ideally after one cycle of high-dose cytarabine. Patients without a donor or who were otherwise ineligible for transplantation were intended to receive four cycles of high-dose cytarabine. Cytarabine dose in consolidation was age-adjusted as described in Figure 6.
L1293: Midostaurin was administered at 50 mg bid starting on day 8 (for induction cycles) or day 6 (for consolidation cycles) through 48h prior to the start of the next chemotherapy cycle. Maintenance therapy (50 mg bid continuously for one year) was intended for all patients, to
L1294: begin immediately after the last consolidation cycle or, at earliest, 30 days after allogeneic HSCT.
L1295: Patients were continuously monitored for adverse events and had blood counts at least 3x weekly and serum chemistries at least weekly during induction and consolidation cycles. An echocardiogram was obtained at baseline and at the end of study. Response assessments were performed after every treatment cycle of induction and consolidation, and every 3 months during maintenance therapy. Response was evaluated using standard International Working Group (IWG) criteria (Cheson et al, JCO 2003).
L1296: ## 6.3.2. Study Results
L1297: ## Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L1298: The study sponsor provided attestation that this study was conducted in accordance with U.S. regulations governing the protection of human subjects, institutional review boards, and the obligations of clinical investigators in accordance with good clinical practice (GCP).
L1299: ## Financial Disclosure
L1300: Study ADE02T was an investigator-sponsored trial. Financial disclosure information for this trial was not provided by the applicant.
L1301: ## Patient Disposition
L1302: A total of 153 subjects enrolled on Study AADE01T. A summary of patient disposition was provided by the applicant and is adapted in Table 15.

L1309: Rydapt® (midostaurin)
L1310: Eight patients (5%) were excluded from all other analyses by the study sponsor, leaving 145 patients evaluated for safety and efficacy. Five patients were excluded for not meeting inclusion criteria; of these, 2 received no study therapy and the other 3 received study therapy but no midostaurin. One patient was excluded after withdrawing informed consent on day 4 of induction therapy, and 2 patients were excluded because no CRF data were available. The sponsor provided no additional data to the FDA regarding these 8 patients.
L1311: ## Demographic Characteristics
L1312: According to an analysis performed by the study sponsor, Study ADE02T enrolled more women (84, 58%) than men (61, 42%). Race and ethnicity data were not collected. The median age of study participants was 54 (range 20-69). More subjects were \le 60 years (99, 68%) than &gt; 60 years (46, 32%).
L1313: ## Other Baseline Characteristics
L1314: According to an analysis performed by the study sponsor, most patients (125, 87%) had de novo AML. AML after MDS and AML after chemo and/or radiation therapy were less frequent (7% and 6%, respectively). Secondary AML was more common in patients age &gt; 60 years (9, 20%) than in patients age \le 60 years (9, 9%). NPM1 mutations were present in 89 patients (61%), with a higher incidence in younger patients (65% vs 54%). The proportion of patients with FLT3 ITD allelic ratio \ge 0.51 was approximately 52% in both age groups.
L1315: ## Efficacy Results - Primary Endpoint
L1316: At the time of the data cutoff, with a median follow-up time of 25.2 months, a total of 95 EFS were observed in the 145 patients. The study sponsor stated that the 2-year EFS was 34.6% for the entire study population (median EFS 10.7 months). The 2-year EFS was 38.2% in patients age \le 60 years (median EFS 13.8 months), and 27.1% in patients age &gt; 60 years (median EFS 9.3 months). A breakdown of observed EFS events provided by the study sponsor is reproduced in Table 16. Older patients were more likely to die during induction therapy (17% vs 3%) and more likely to relapse (28% vs 18%) than younger patients. There was no difference in EFS between patients with high and low FLT3-ITD allelic ratio, regardless of whether the cutoff used was 0.7 or 0.51.

L1331: 1 The applicant did not provide the breakdown by CR and CRi, nor did they specify whether the indicated number included patients who required a second induction therapy.
L1332: 2 A total of 74 OS events were observed, 46 in patients age ≤ 60 and 28 in patients age &gt; 60.
L1333: **Reviewer comment:** Study A2301 excluded patients age &gt; 60 due to the presumption that older patients with AML should not receive intensive chemotherapy. However, intensive chemotherapy is increasingly being used successfully in fit older patients with AML, and age by itself is no longer considered an acceptable reason to disqualify a patient from receiving it (Ferrara et al, Leukemia 2013). The FDA analyzed data summarized in the CSR from Study ADE02T to determine whether it would help support an indication for midostaurin in untreated AML without age restriction, even though the pivotal study was conducted in patients age &lt; 60 years.
L1334: Time-to-event endpoints cannot be interpreted on single arm trials, and since CR rate was lumped together with CRI rate, the data as presented in the ADE02T CSR are challenging to compare with historical controls. However, the difference in 2-year OS between patients who were \le 60 years and those who were &gt; 60 years was just 8.5%, which compares favorably to the approximately 20% difference in 2-year OS between patients age \le 60 and &gt; 60 who were treated with intensive chemotherapy in a large retrospective review of patients in France treated between January 1, 2000 and December 31, 2009 (Bertolli et al, Blood 2013). It also compares favorably to the estimated 30% difference in 2-year OS between the patients age \le 60 and &gt; 60 that has been estimated from literature reviews (Fernandez et al, NEJM 2009 and Estey, JCO 2006). As there does not appear to be a decrease in expected 2-year survival in younger patients compared to historical controls (with the caveat regarding interpretation of time-to-event endpoints noted earlier), it is unlikely that this narrowing of expected OS between the two age groups is due to underperformance of the treatment regimen in the younger patients on Study ADE02T.
L1335: This study thus provides limited evidence that the addition of midostaurin to intensive chemotherapy does not appear to adversely impact efficacy in patients age &gt; 60 years, and in fact may improve it, and that it may therefore be reasonable to extrapolate survival data from Study A2301 to a broader patient population.
L1336: ## 6.4. Studies A2104, A2104E1, and A2014E2
L1337: ## 6.4.1. Study Design
L1338: ## Study A2104
L1339: Study A2014 was a phase II, open-label proof-of-concept trial of midostaurin monotherapy in patients with FLT3-mutated AML or MDS. The study population included patients age \ge 18 years with untreated AML who were not candidates for myelosuppressive chemotherapy, patients with relapsed or refractory AML, and patients with high risk MDS. Patients who had undergone HSCT within the last 2 months or had had more than 2 prior regimens were ineligible.
L1340: A total of 20 patients (18 with AML and 2 with MDS) were treated on this study between January 2002 and September 2003 at 3 study sites in the United States. Of these, 7 (35%) had untreated disease. All received midostaurin monotherapy at a dose of 75 mg TID until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. Safety evaluations were conducted at least weekly, and marrow evaluations were performed at the beginning of every 28-day treatment cycle and at the time of treatment discontinuation. The primary efficacy endpoint was investigator-determined best clinical response, according to National Cancer Institute definitions for AML (Cheson 1990) and WHO-IWG for MDS (Cheson, 2000). The study is complete.
L1341: ## Clinical Review
L1342: Ashley Ward, MD
L1343: NDA 207997
L1344: Rydapt® (midostaurin)
L1345: ## Study A2104E1
L1346: Study A2014E1 was conducted as an extension of study A2014 (via protocol amendment #3). It was a phase II, open-label, randomized trial of midostaurin monotherapy in patients with AML or MDS. The study population was virtually identical to that enrolled on Study A2014, except that patients with either wild type or mutant FLT3 were eligible and patients were not allowed to have received a prior FLT3 inhibitor.
L1347: A total of 95 patients (85 with AML and 10 with MDS) were treated on this study between March 2003 and September 2004 at 4 study sites in the United States and 2 in Germany. Of these, 52 patients (55%) had previously untreated disease (12 with FLT3 mutations and 40 with wild type FLT3). Patients were randomized 1:1 to receive either 50 mg or 100 mg of midostaurin bid in continuous, 28-day cycles. Safety evaluations were conducted at least weekly, and marrow evaluations were performed at the beginning of every 28-day treatment cycle and at the time of treatment discontinuation. The primary efficacy endpoint was investigator-determined best clinical response measured on or after cycle 1, day 8, according to NCI definitions for AML (Cheson 1990) and WHO-IWG for MDS (Cheson, 2000). The study is complete.
L1348: ## Study A2104E2
L1349: Study A2014E2 was conducted as a second extension of study A2014 (via protocol amendment #4). It was a phase I/II, open-label, proof-of-concept trial of midostaurin monotherapy administered via intra-patient dose escalation or in combination with itraconazole in patients with AML or MDS. The study population was virtually identical to that enrolled on Study A2014, except that patients with either wild type or mutant FLT3 were eligible and patients were not allowed to have received a prior FLT3 inhibitor.
L1350: A total of 29 patients (all with AML) were treated on this study between August 2003 and March 2008 at 3 study sites in the United States and 2 in Germany. Of these, 16 patients (55%) had previously untreated disease (5 with FLT3 mutations and 11 with wild type FLT3). Sixteen of the patients (including 2 who had been receiving therapy on Study A2104E1 at the time of the amendment) were enrolled on the midostaurin dose escalation arm, in which patients started midostaurin at 100 mg bid for two weeks, then, in the absence of Grade 3 or 4 adverse events, increased their dose by 50mg bid every two weeks to a maximum dose of 300 mg bid. Midostaurin monotherapy was continued until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. The other 13 patients were enrolled to evaluate the effects of combining midostaurin with itraconazole. These patients received midostaurin at 100 mg bid for the first two days, followed by 50 mg bid. On cycle 1, day 22, itraconazole was started at a dose of 100 mg bid. Both study medications were continued until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. Safety evaluations were conducted at least weekly, and marrow evaluations were performed at the beginning of every 28-day treatment cycle and at the time of treatment discontinuation. The
L1351: ## Clinical Review
L1352: Ashley Ward, MD
L1353: NDA 207997
L1354: Rydapt® (midostaurin)
L1355: primary efficacy endpoint was investigator-determined best clinical response measured on or after cycle 1, day 8, according to NCI definitions for AML (Cheson 1990) and WHO-IWG for MDS (Cheson, 2000). The study is complete.
L1356: ## 6.4.2. Study Results
L1357: ## Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L1358: The applicant provided attestation that these studies were conducted in accordance with U.S. regulations governing the protection of human subjects, institutional review boards, and the obligations of clinical investigators in accordance with good clinical practice (GCP).
L1359: ## Financial Disclosure
L1360: A summary of financial disclosures for Studies A2104, A2104E1 and A2014E2 are provided in the appendix (Section 13.2). The applicant was unable to obtain financial disclosure information from 1 U.S. sub-investigator. The principal investigator at one study site (b) (6) on Study A2104E1 reported a grant in excess of $25,000 from the study sponsor to the investigator to fund ongoing research. His study site enrolled (b) (6) of the 95 subjects (b) (6)% on Study A2014E1. No financial interests or arrangements were reported in the other investigators.
L1361: ## Patient Disposition
L1362: On Study A2104, the median duration of treatment was 35.5 days. Nine patients (45%) received a maximum of one cycle of midostaurin. Only one patient received \ge 6 cycles of midostaurin. All patients discontinued the study, the majority due to lack of efficacy (n=13, 65%).
L1363: On Study A2104E1, the median duration of treatment was 46 days in FLT3 mutated patients and 53 days in FLT3 wild type patients. Most patients (n=55, 58%) received 1-2 cycles of midostaurin. Twelve patients received \ge 6 cycles of midostaurin. All patients discontinued the study except for the two patients that were rolled over onto Study A2104E2. The most frequent reason for discontinuation was lack of efficacy (n=62, 65%).
L1364: On Study A2104E2, the median duration of treatment was 29 days in the intrapatient dose escalation arm and 24 days in the midostaurin + itraconazole arm. Most patients (n=16, 55%) received 1-2 cycles of midostaurin. Six patients received \ge 6 cycles of midostaurin. All patients discontinued the study, primarily due to lack of efficacy (13, 45%) or adverse events (8, 28%).

L1370: Reviewer comment: Studies A2104, A2104E1 and A2104E2 provide substantial evidence of lack of efficacy of midostaurin monotherapy in patients with de novo and relapsed/refractory AML.
L1371: ## 7 Integrated Review of Effectiveness
L1372: ## 7.1. Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
L1373: ## 7.1.1. Primary Endpoints
L1374: Midostaurin, administered at 50 mg by mouth twice daily on days 8-21 of each cycle of induction (daunorubicin and cytarabine) and consolidation (high-dose cytarabine) chemotherapy to patients with previously untreated FLT3+ AML confers a survival advantage when compared to placebo (HR 0.77, p=0.016). Due to plateauing of the OS curves, estimates of median OS are not reliable, but the restricted mean survival advantage ranges from 2.6 - 5.4 months depending on time of follow-up. Although the pivotal study was conducted in patients age &lt; 60, data from an investigator-sponsored trial of midostaurin in combination with intensive chemotherapy in older adults with AML lend support for a broad indication without age restriction.
L1375: Data from several studies consistently showed that midostaurin does not produce any complete responses when administered as monotherapy across a wide range of doses in patients with previously untreated or relapsed/refractory AML.
L1376: ## 7.1.2. Secondary and Other Endpoints
L1377: In the pivotal study, event-free survival (8.2 months vs 3.6 months) and rates of complete response by day 60 (59% vs 54%) were both also higher on the midostaurin arm, revealing consistent benefit of midostaurin in combination with chemotherapy in untreated AML.
L1378: ## 7.1.3. Dose and Dose-Response
L1379: Study A2106 was the only dose-ranging study in patients with previously untreated AML. As summarized in Section 6.2.2, administration of midostaurin at 50 mg bid on days 8-21 of each cycle of induction and consolidation chemotherapy was better tolerated and more effective than higher doses or when administered concomitantly (during week 1) with chemotherapy. Literature search reveals biologic support for these observations, independent of toxicity: experiments with FLT3 mutated cell lines treated with lestaurtinib (a multikinase inhibitor somewhat related to midostaurin), showed synergy when lestaurtinib was given in combination with or after chemotherapy agents, but antagonism when administered before chemotherapy agents (Levis et al Blood 2003). Dose modifications for toxicity or lack of efficacy were not studied in patients with previously untreated AML eligible for standard intensive chemotherapy.
L1380: ## 7.1.4. Onset, Duration, and Durability of Efficacy Effects
L1381: Most of the CRs that were observed on Study A2301 occurred after 1 or 2 cycles of induction, which is typical for intensive chemotherapy regimens. Duration of response is relatively uninformative since this trial had a high rate of patients who went on to HSCT, but the study demonstrated a statistically significant overall survival advantage of 2.6 to 5.4 months (depending on follow-up time) with the addition of midostaurin to a standard chemotherapy regimen in previously untreated AML. See Section 6.2.2 for a discussion of the lack of evidence for continuing midostaurin beyond induction and consolidation therapy in this indication.
L1382: ## 7.2. Additional Efficacy Considerations
L1383: ## 7.2.1. Considerations on Benefit in the Postmarket Setting
L1384: While patients who enroll on clinical trials tend to differ in various ways from the broader population of patients with cancer, there is no indication that efficacy would be lower in patient populations underrepresented on Study A2301. As patients receiving intensive induction and consolidation therapy for AML are typically treated as inpatients, compliance in the post-market setting is expected to be high.
L1385: ## 7.3. Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
L1386: The efficacy of midostaurin in AML has been established, based primarily on the results of a single randomized, phase III study in patients age 18-60 with previously untreated FLT3+ AML, in which patients who received 50 mg bid of midostaurin sequentially in combination with intensive chemotherapy had longer overall survival than patients who received placebo (HR 0.77, p=0.019). The median OS estimates are uninformative due to plateau of the survival curves, but the RMST analysis demonstrates that midostaurin confers a survival advantage of approximately 2.6 to 5.4 months. As the study did not re-randomize patients at the start of
L1387: maintenance therapy, and the OS benefit appears to be explained primarily by treatment effects observed during the period of intensive chemotherapy, Study A2301 does not support maintenance dosing of midostaurin.
L1388: The dose and regimen used in the pivotal study are supported by the data from Study A2106, in which patients who received higher doses of midostaurin (100 mg bid) in combination with chemotherapy experienced worse outcomes. Although Study A2301 was restricted to patients age \le 60 years, the expansion of the indication to include adult patients who are candidates for intensive chemotherapy regardless of age is supported by the data from Study ADE02T, in which patients age &gt; 60 appeared to have improved survival with the proposed regimen relative to patients age \le 60, compared to historical data. Data from Studies A2014, A2014E1 and A2104E2 demonstrate lack of efficacy of midostaurin as monotherapy in patients with de novo and relapsed/refractory AML, and support a limitation of use statement in the product label.
L1389: ## 8 Review of Safety
L1390: ## 8.1. Safety Review Approach
L1391: Although review emphasis was placed on safety data from Study A2301, results from all 24 trials listed in Table 2 were used to support the analysis of safety. Pooled safety data from all trials of midostaurin monotherapy and the two trials of midostaurin in combination with intensive chemotherapy for AML are summarized where relevant in Section 8.4.
L1392: Several studies for which databases were not available were also considered relevant for the proposed indication. Patients with previously untreated AML on Study ADE02T (n=143) as well as 22 healthy volunteers on Study 001 and 1 healthy volunteer on study A2120 were exposed to midostaurin on these studies. While they are not included in the pooled analysis, portions of the clinical study reports from these studies are also summarized where relevant in Section 8.4.

L1424: Due to the substantial amount of missing baseline laboratory data, missing normal lab ranges, and implausible lab values noted in the laboratory datasets provided by the applicant, it was not possible to determine the number of patients on Study A2301 and in the pooled safety database that had baseline hepatic or renal dysfunction.
L1425: ## 8.2.3. Adequacy of the safety database:
L1426: The size of the safety database is adequate to provide a reasonable estimate of adverse reactions that may be observed with midostaurin, and the duration of treatment is adequate to allow assessment of adverse reactions over time. Study A2301 randomizes midostaurin against placebo, which is very helpful in understanding the contribution of the underlying disease to adverse reactions. Study A2301 and the safety pool are representative of typical patients with AML that participate on clinical trials. However, non-white patients are under-represented compared to the overall AML population in the United States. Please see section 8.3.1 for a discussion of the quality of the safety database.
L1427: ## 8.3. Adequacy of Applicant's Clinical Safety Assessments
L1428: ## 8.3.1. Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
L1429: On the pivotal study A2301, the collection of adverse events and severe adverse events differed between clinical trial sites in North America and those in other regions due to differences in sponsorship. This discrepancy in adverse event collection was not pre-specified by the protocol. Clinical trial sites in North American collected all grade \ge 3 adverse events, but only collected grade 1 and 2 adverse events for 13 pre-specified adverse events (neutrophils/granulocytes, platelets, hemoglobin, febrile neutropenia, ataxia, rash/desquamation, diarrhea, nausea, vomiting, keratitis, fatigue, left ventricular systolic dysfunction and mucositis/stomatitis). Clinical trial sites outside of North America collected all adverse events regardless of grade. Adverse events, regardless of clinical trial site, were recorded by reporting period (cycle) but start and stop dates were not collected. If an event happened intermittently or repeatedly within one reporting period, the site was only required to report the worse National Cancer Institute Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events (NCI-CTCAE) grade observed for that event during the reporting period. Seriousness of the adverse events was not captured by the investigator on the CRF, although serious adverse events (with the exception of myelosuppression, febrile neutropenia, nausea, vomiting and hospitalization resulting from these) were required to be reported to the study sponsor and were collected in the Novartis safety database.
L1430: The collection of post-baseline laboratory values was initiated after the start of the study, and the data were collected retrospectively for only those sites outside of North America. The database of laboratory values provided by the sponsor is of poor quality, with many missing baseline values, missing normal lab ranges, and implausible lab values recorded.
L1431: ## 8.3.2. Categorization of Adverse Events
L1432: Adverse events (AE) were coded by the Investigators in the CRF using the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) version 6.0, and re-coded by Novartis using the latest MedDRA version 18.1. Adverse events were graded by the investigators in the CRF using the NCI-CTCAE for AEs version 3.0 until Oct-2011, and thereafter using version 4.0. The seriousness for each AE was not recorded in the CRF.
L1433: The FDA compared the verbatim adverse event term with the MedDRA preferred term for all adverse events reported on Study A2301 and did not identify any irregularities. The applicant did not group preferred terms in their analysis. The FDA grouped related preferred terms for all analyses; a listing of these grouped terms can be found in Appendix 13.3. Standard MedDra Query (SMQ) analysis was performed using MAED, and no additional safety signals were identified beyond those discussed below.
L1434: ## 8.3.3. Routine Clinical Tests
L1435: See Section 6 for a description of the frequency of clinical assessments for the studies conducted in patients with AML. The frequency of clinical assessments in the other studies included in the safety pool varied, but is adequate to assess the risks of serious safety events.
L1436: ## 8.4. Safety Results
L1437: ## 8.4.1. Deaths
L1438: The FDA identified 398 deaths among the 1,298 subjects exposed to midostaurin (Table 23). Most deaths occurred more than 28-30 days after the last dose of midostaurin (304 subjects, 23%), leaving 94 on-treatment deaths.

L1443: The applicant identified the underlying malignancy as the cause of death in 46 of the 94 on-treatment deaths (49%) in subjects exposed to midostaurin. L1444: The FDA identified 347 deaths among the 680 subjects on Study A2301 who received at least one dose of midostaurin or placebo. Most deaths occurred more than 30 days after the last dose of midostaurin (147 subjects, 43%) or placebo (164 subjects, 49%) (Table 24).

L1449: Ashley Ward, MD
L1450: NDA 207997
L1451: Rydapt® (midostaurin)
L1452: The FDA reviewed individual patient narratives from the remaining 36 subjects to confirm the cause of on-treatment deaths. Narratives were not available for deaths that occurred more than 30 days after the last dose of midostaurin. In general, the narratives were poorly written, consisting of a reiteration in paragraph form of labs, adverse events and concomitant medications already provided in data sets. In patients with AML, particularly those treated with intensive chemotherapy, root cause of death early in the course of treatment was generally difficult to determine due to the high incidence of baseline comorbid conditions, many caused by the underlying disease. With those caveats, the FDA concluded that 12 of the 15 deaths (80%) on the midostaurin arm and 14 of the 21 of the deaths (67%) on the placebo arm were likely attributable to complications of infection (Table 25). The other three patients on the midostaurin arm died from pulmonary hemorrhage, progressive disease and hemorrhagic colitis, none of which are unusual causes of death in patients with AML receiving intensive chemotherapy.

L1462: On study ADE02T, 16 patients died during treatment with midostaurin or in the 30-day follow-up period (2 of these deaths occurred prior to initiation of midostaurin). Two patients died in maintenance, one of a hemorrhagic stroke and the other of relapsed disease. The other 14 patients died during induction (n=12) or consolidation (n=2). Cause of death in these patients was infection (n=9), hemorrhagic stroke (n=2), pulmonary hemorrhage (n=1), myocardial ischemia (n=1), or sudden cardiac death (n=1). While more patients age &gt; 60 years died during study treatment (n=10, 22%) than patients age ≤ 60 (n=6, 6%), the addition of midostaurin to intensive chemotherapy did not appear to increase the early death rate in older patients compared to the 11-22% observed on other studies (Brandwein et al, Am J Blood Res 2013).
L1463: ## 8.4.2. Serious Adverse Events
L1464: The seriousness of adverse events was not captured on CRFs by the investigators on Study A2301. Instead, serious adverse events (SAE) were reported individually to the study sponsor who captured them in a separate safety database. For this submission, the applicant manually reconciled adverse events in the clinical trial database with those reported in the company's safety database to flag events in the clinical trial database that were reported as serious. Myelosuppression, febrile neutropenia, nausea, vomiting and hospitalization from these were exempt from reporting, and thus are not included in the applicant's analysis of SAE. The applicant performed several sensitivity analyses in an effort to mitigate the inherent risk of under-reporting of SAE, including all non-reconciled grade 3-4 adverse events in the clinical data base without a match in the safety database (excluding those exempt from reporting as described above) and all of the non-reconciled adverse events in the safety database. The applicant stated that results of these sensitivity analyses did not change their conclusion that the overall incidence of serious adverse events, the incidence of serious infections, and the incidence of serious episodes of pneumonia and pneumonitis were similar between the two treatment groups (Study A2301 Clinical Study Report Section 12.3.6 dated July 5, 2016).
L1465: ## Clinical Review
L1466: Ashley Ward, MD
L1467: NDA 207997
L1468: Rydapt® (midostaurin)
L1469: The applicant identified 34 fatal adverse events in 30 subjects that occurred on study therapy or within 30 days after the last dose of midostaurin/placebo on Study A2301. Fourteen subjects (4%) on the midostaurin arm and 16 subjects (5%) on the placebo arm experienced at least one fatal adverse event. The most commonly reported fatal adverse events on the midostaurin arm were sepsis (4 subjects), colitis (3 subjects) and multiorgan failure (2 subjects). The most commonly reported fatal adverse events on the placebo arm were sepsis (6 subjects) and multiorgan failure (3 subjects).
L1470: FDA performed an analysis of serious adverse events reported in the clinical trial database (which is subject to the limitations described above) using grouped preferred terms (see Appendix Section 13.3). The most frequently occurring (\ge 5\%) serious adverse events were febrile neutropenia (107, 16%), sepsis (67 (10%), neutrophil count decreased (62, 9%), pneumonia (60, 9%), platelet count decreased (54, 8%), diarrhea (40, 6%), and device-related infection (40, 6%). The only serious adverse events occurring \ge 2\% more frequently on the midostaurin arm than the placebo arm were device-related infection (7% vs 4%), hypotension (4% vs 1%), hypertransaminasemia (3% vs 1%), rash (3% vs 1%) and pneumonitis (6% vs 4%).
L1471: The FDA did not analyze serious adverse events in the pooled safety database, as the seriousness of events was not collected consistently across studies.
L1472: On study ADE02T, 94 patients (65%) experienced a serious adverse event. Serious adverse events were modestly more frequent in patients age &gt; 60 years (n=33, 72%) than in those \le 60 years of age (n=61, 62%). SAEs occurring in \ge 5\% of patients were sepsis (n=11), renal failure (n=10), pyrexia (n=9), diarrhea (n=8), prolonged QT interval (n=7), ALT elevation (n=7) and platelet count decreased (n=7).

Adverse events leading to discontinuation in more than one patient on the midostaurin arm are listed in Table 26, in order of frequency on the midostaurin arm. The most frequent reasons patients discontinued study treatment on the midostaurin arm were elevated transaminases (n=4), rash (n=4) and vomiting (n=3).

L1517: The results of this analysis demonstrate a safety profile generally consistent with that observed on Study A2301, with the exception of cough and dyspnea, which were not prominent toxicities on the midostaurin arm compared to the placebo arm on Study A2301. The applicant proposed a Warning and Precaution of pulmonary toxicity / interstitial lung disease. In a response (dated December 29, 2016, SDN 61) to an FDA information request, the applicant reported 57 cases of possible interstitial lung disease from patients in the safety pool based on an Interstitial Lung Disease MedDRA standardized query. The majority of these cases (37, 65%), and all but one of the grade ≥ 3 events, occurred on Study A2301. FDA analysis of the safety data from Study A2301 using grouped terms did not reveal an imbalance between treatment arms with respect to pneumonitis (12% in both arms) or pneumonia (17% on the midostaurin arm and 18% on the placebo arm). All other cases occurred on other studies of midostaurin in patients with AML, advanced systemic mastocytosis, or mast cell leukemia.
L1518: **Reviewer comment:** Although lung toxicity does not appear to be a prominent toxicity of midostaurin in patients with untreated AML based on findings from Study A2301, given the
L1519: lung findings observed in the nonclinical toxicology findings, and the observation of respiratory symptoms (cough, dyspnea) in the pooled safety analysis, this reviewer is not opposed to the sponsor's proposal of a Warning and Precaution for possible pulmonary toxicity in the product label.
L1520: ## 8.4.5. Laboratory Findings
L1521: As the laboratory datasets submitted by the applicant were poorly defined, and missing many test results, normal ranges and test grades, and there appeared to be inconsistent application of the implausible result flag, the FDA was unable to independently analyze all laboratory findings on Study A2301 and in the safety pool. However, the FDA reproduced the analysis for selected lab abnormalities and results were generally consistent with those of the applicant.
L1522: On Study A2301, the applicant found that the only chemistry abnormality that was new or worsening in at least 10% of patients and at least 2% more frequently on the midostaurin arm was hypernatremia (21% vs 16%). Although the frequency of all grade ALT elevation was virtually identical on the midostaurin and placebo arms (69% vs 68%, respectively), the frequency of grade \ge 3 ALT elevation was higher on the midostaurin arm (20% vs 16%). During induction and consolidation, most patients on both arms experienced profound pancytopenia, as would be expected, and there were no differences between the treatment arms.
L1523: The applicant did a more in-depth analysis of newly occurring liver function abnormalities on Study A2301, reproduced in Table 31. While there appears to be a slight increase in frequency of ALT or AST elevations \le 8\times ULN on the midostaurin arm compared to the placebo arm, ALT or AST elevations &gt; 8\times ULN and cases of Hy's law appear slightly higher on the placebo arm. According to the sponsor's analysis, only two patients (one on each arm) experienced ALT or AST elevations &gt; 8\times ULN during maintenance, and none experienced elevations &gt; 10\times ULN.

L1529: ## 8.4.6. Vital Signs
L1530: The Applicant did not collect vital signs, other than weight, on Study A2301.
L1531: In their integrated analysis of safety, the applicant described vital sign changes in patients with aggressive systemic mastocytosis or mast cell leukemia on studies D2201 and A2213. According to the applicant, the only notable finding was a transient decrease in diastolic blood pressure in the first 3 months of treatment, which appeared to correlate with an increased incidence of hypotension and dizziness reported in the first 6 months of treatment.
L1532: The FDA used the pooled safety database to evaluate vital signs in patients exposed to midostaurin on studies other than A2301. Although the safety pool is somewhat skewed given the inclusion of healthy volunteers, and the analysis is limited to due lack of randomized comparison with placebo, there do not appear to be any vital sign abnormalities in patients treated with midostaurin beyond what would be expected in the general population (Table 32). With the exception of lower heart rate in younger patients and in men, and higher blood pressure in older patients, which are all consistent with the general population, there does not appear to be an age- or gender-specific impact of midostaurin on vital signs.

L1538: ## 8.4.7. Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
L1539: ECGs were obtained at baseline, several times per cycle during induction and consolidation, and at the beginning of each cycle of maintenance therapy on Study A2301. Arrhythmias (most frequently sinus tachycardia (n=60, 9%) were noted in 43 patients (12%) on the midostaurin arm and 54 patients (16%) on the placebo arm. Myocardial ischemia was reported in 8 patients (2%) on the midostaurin arm and 6 patients (2%) on the placebo arm. All other ECG-related adverse events reported were rare (occurring in ≤ 4 patients), and were evenly distributed on both study arms.
L1540: ## 8.4.8. QT
L1541: According to the applicant, midostaurin and its active metabolites (CGP62221 and CGP52421) were evaluated in vitro in the human ether-a-go-go (hERG) assay for the potential to cause QTc prolongation. The hERG IC50 for midostaurin and its metabolites could not be determined at the limit of solubility. As the concentrations tested in vitro exceed the levels that patients would be exposed to, the applicant concluded that the risk of QTc interval prolongation at therapeutic exposures is considered low. Long-term toxicology studies in dogs revealed some cardiotoxicity (decreased heart rate, myocardial degeneration, myocarditis and P-Q prolongation) but no effects of midostaurin on the QT interval.
L1542: The FDA Interdisciplinary Review Team (IRT) for QT Studies reviewed the thorough QT study. The effect of midostaurin (75 mg BID for 3 days) on the QTc interval was evaluated in a randomized, placebo and moxifloxacin controlled, multiple-dose, blinded, parallel study. There was no clinically significant prolongation of QTc interval or relationship between changes in QTc and concentrations for midostaurin and its active metabolites (CGP62221 and CGP52421). The study duration was not long enough to estimate the effects of the metabolite CGP52421 on the QT/QTc interval. The IRT concluded that there is no evidence that midostaurin affects the QTc interval.
L1543: In a randomized placebo-controlled study in patients with AML (Study A2301), the proportion of patients with QTc prolongation was higher in patients randomized to midostaurin as compared to placebo (QTcF &gt; 480 ms: 10.1% vs 5.7%; QTcF &gt; 500 ms: 6.2% vs 2.6%; \DeltaQTcF &gt; 60 ms: 18.4% vs 10.7%). However, as information collected about concomitant medications did not include reliable start and stop dates, it is difficult to assess the impact of concomitant medication use on these findings.
L1544: Reviewer comment: Given that the thorough QT study had a minor limitation in that it was not long enough to estimate the effects of the metabolite CGP52421 on the QT interval, and that many patients with AML receive concomitant therapies that may prolong the QT interval, it would be reasonable to recommend QT monitoring as part of the intensive inpatient monitoring that AML patients receive while undergoing therapy.
L1545: ## 8.5. Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
L1546: ## 8.5.1. Embryo-fetal toxicity
L1547: Animal reproductive studies of midostaurin demonstrated that midostaurin caused embryo-fetal toxicities, including late embryo-fetal death and reduced fetal birth weight, with delays in fetal growth at doses lower than the recommended human dose. As the chemotherapy regimen used with midostaurin in Study A2301 is used in pregnant women during their second and third trimesters, it will be important to remind prescribers that midostaurin should not be
L1548: administered to pregnant women. After consultation with the Division of Maternal and Pediatric Health (DMPH), we recommend a boxed warning. See Section 8.8.2 for further details.
L1549: ## 8.5.2. Nausea and vomiting
L1550: Nausea and vomiting are frequent adverse events associated with intensive chemotherapy for AML, but the addition of midostaurin appears to increase their frequency. On Study A2301, 83% of the patients on the midostaurin arm reported nausea (6% grade \ge 3) compared to 74% of the patients on the placebo arm (10% grade \ge 3). Similarly, 62% of the patients on the midostaurin arm reported vomiting (3% grade \ge 3) compared to 57% of the patients on the placebo arm (4% \ge grade 4). This effect of midostaurin was more noticeable in the maintenance phase of the clinical trial, after completion of intensive chemotherapy, where the rate of nausea was 53% (22% on the placebo arm) and the rate of vomiting was 24% (compared to 11% on the placebo arm). While these events were generally low-grade and did not prompt frequent study treatment discontinuations, prescribers should be encouraged to use anti-emetic therapy in patients taking midostaurin.
L1551: ## 8.6. Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
L1552: As reporting of race and ethnicity were sparse across all trials, safety was not analyzed by these variables. An analysis of TEAEs by gender on Study A2301 is summarized in Table 33, in order of the magnitude in difference in rate of the TEAE between the two genders. Vomiting, diarrhea, nausea, abdominal pain and rash were more common in females than males on both treatment arms, as were events physiologically associated with female gender (vaginal hemorrhage, urinary tract infection, and menorrhagia). The delta between rates for men or women was similar on both treatment arms, suggesting that there are no toxicities from midostaurin that are highly specific to women.
L1553: Of the adverse events observed more commonly in males on the midostaurin arm, several were observed less commonly in males on the placebo arm, raising the possibility of toxicities from midostaurin that are more specific to men. PTT prolongation, decreased appetite and arrhythmias were particularly pronounced in this respect. However, as overall survival benefit in men who received midostaurin was, if anything, better than that of women on Study A2301, the clinical significance of this post-hoc subgroup analysis is dubious.

L1557: ## 8.7. Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
L1558: No studies or trials were conducted to evaluate a specific safety concern.
L1559: ## 8.8. Additional Safety Explorations
L1560: ## 8.8.1. Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
L1561: A formal human carcinogenicity study was not conducted for midostaurin. Neoplasms (identified using the Neoplasms, Benign, Malignant and Unspecified SOC) were rare on Study A2301. 6 patients (2%) on the midostaurin arm and 6 (2%) on the placebo arm developed a neoplasm. Most were benign. Second primary malignancies (colon cancer, myelodysplastic syndrome, and a testicular neoplasm) developed in 3 patients on the placebo arm. A second
L1562: primary malignancy (pineal neoplasm) was reported in only one patient on midostaurin. Based on these data, no secondary cancer safety signal was identified.
L1563: ## 8.8.2. Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
L1564: In animal reproduction studies, oral administration of midostaurin to pregnant rats and rabbits during organogenesis caused embryo-fetal toxicities, including late embryo-fetal death and reduced fetal birth weight, with delays in fetal growth at doses lower than the recommended human dose. The DMPH reviewer identified 8 pregnancies on subjects in clinical trials of midostaurin. Three of these patients reported definite or possible fetal exposure to midostaurin and underwent elective abortions at \le 12 weeks gestational age. Fetal outcomes were not reported. One patient delivered a healthy term baby 2 years after the last intake of midostaurin. The remaining 4 patients did not have fetal exposure to midostaurin (3 received placebo, 1 was pregnant at diagnosis and began treatment after delivery).
L1565: DMPH recommends that a boxed warning for embryo-fetal toxicity be added to labeling for midostaurin and recommends verification of pregnancy status prior to treatment in females of reproductive potential. Since the “7+3” regimen is the most common regimen used in pregnant women in their 2nd and 3rd trimesters, this reviewer agree with their recommendation. DMPH also recommends effective contraception during treatment and for 4 months following the last dose of midostaurin.
L1566: Midostaurin may also adversely impact male fertility based on nonclinical findings as reviewed in Section 4.3.
L1567: ## 8.8.3. Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L1568: The applicant was granted Orphan Designation for midostaurin for the treatment of patients with AML and is therefore exempt from pediatric studies under the Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA). (b) (4)
L1569: ## 8.8.4. Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
L1570: The applicant did not provide any reported cases of overdose of midostaurin in the AML population. Midostaurin does not have abuse potential.
L1571: ## 8.9. Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L1572: ## 8.9.1. Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L1573: Midostaurin is not marketed in any country, and there is no postmarket experience.
L1574: ## 8.9.2. Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L1575: Safety in the postmarket setting is expected to be similar to that observed on the clinical trials reviewed in this Application.
L1576: ## 8.10. Additional Safety Issues From Other Disciplines
L1577: No additional safety issues were identified by other disciplines.
L1578: ## 8.11. Integrated Assessment of Safety
L1579: The primary data in support of safety for the proposed indication came from Study A2301, in which 345 patients with previously untreated AML were exposed to midostaurin + chemotherapy, and 335 were exposed to placebo + chemotherapy. The median duration of exposure to midostaurin on this study was 1.4 months (mean 4.5 months), with a maximum exposure time of 18.9 months. Data from an additional 953 patients who were exposed to midostaurin across the development program was included in the integrated safety database.
L1580: The study population was monitored for deaths, serious adverse events, common adverse events, and common laboratory tests. A thorough QT study was conducted. Fourteen patients on the midostaurin arm died during or within 30 days after study treatment (compared to 21 on the placebo arm). The most frequent cause of death on both arms was infection, which is typical in this patient population and is considered related to the underlying disease.
L1581: Midostaurin was generally well-tolerated. The most common non-laboratory TEAEs (incidence \ge 20% and \ge 2% more frequent on the midostaurin arm) were rash, vomiting, mucositis, headache, musculoskeletal pain, petechiae, device-related infection and epistaxis. The most frequent Grade 3/4 adverse reactions (incidence \ge 10% and \ge 2% more frequent on the midostaurin arm) were febrile neutropenia, diarrhea, device-related infection, sepsis, rash, pneumonia and mucositis. The most frequent serious adverse reaction was febrile neutropenia, which occurred at similar rates in the midostaurin (16%) and placebo arms (16%).
L1582: Gastrointestinal toxicities were more noticeable on the maintenance phase of the study, in which midostaurin/placebo was administered as a monotherapy. In maintenance 53% of patients on the midostaurin arm reported nausea (vs 22% on the placebo arm). Other adverse events occurring in \ge 20% of the patients in the midostaurin arm and \ge 2% more frequently than in patients on the placebo arm were diarrhea (30% vs 22%), fatigue (30% vs 28%), elevated transaminases (28% vs 19%) and vomiting (24% vs 11%). Almost all of these events were mild-moderate in severity.
L1583: ## Clinical Review
L1584: Ashley Ward, MD
L1585: NDA 207997
L1586: Rydapt® (midostaurin)
L1587: Discontinuation due to adverse reaction occurred in 8% of patients on the midostaurin arm versus 5% on the placebo arm. The most frequent Grade 3/4 adverse reactions leading to discontinuation of midostaurin were elevated transaminases (1.4%) and exfoliative dermatitis (1.2%).
L1588: Study ADE02T provided data supporting the use of midostaurin in combination with intensive chemotherapy in older patients with untreated AML. On this study, serious adverse events were modestly more frequent in patients age &gt; 60 years (33, 72%) than in those ≤ 60 years of age (61, 62%). Discontinuations for adverse events were also modestly more frequent in patients age &gt; 60 years (15, 33%) than in those ≤ 60 years of age (26, 27%). Given the historically poor overall survival in older patients with AML, this level of toxicity is acceptable for the clinical benefit observed.

L1669: Kaplan-Meier plot showing Duration of Response (DOR) in Months for three subtypes: ASM (N=16), MCL (N=16), and SM-AHN (N=57). The Y-axis ranges from 0.0 to 1.0. The X-axis ranges from 0 to 60 Months. ASM and MCL show high initial response rates (around 0.9) which stabilize around 0.8 after 12 months. SM-AHN shows a rapid decline in response rate, starting near 1.0 and dropping below 0.3 by 36 months.

L1671: Figure 4 is a Kaplan-Meier plot showing Progression-Free Survival (PFS) Probability versus Months for three subtypes in the D2201 PEP cohort using Modified Valent Criteria. The X-axis represents Months (0 to 60), and the Y-axis represents PFS Probability (0.0 to 1.0).
The legend indicates the subtypes and sample sizes:
ASM (N = 16) (Red line)
MCL (N = 16) (Green line)
SM-AHN (N = 57) (Blue line)
The plot shows that the ASM group maintains the highest PFS probability, followed by MCL, and then SM-AHN. The SM-AHN group shows the steepest decline in PFS probability over time.
L1672: Figure 5 is a Kaplan-Meier plot showing Overall Survival (OS) probability versus Months after Treatment Initiation (0 to 72 months) for patients in the FAS population of Study D2201, stratified by diagnosis. The legend indicates three groups: ASM (N = 16), MCL (N = 21), and SM-AHN (N = 73).
The ASM group (red line) shows the highest survival probability, starting around 0.95 and dropping to approximately 0.55 by 48 months. The SM-AHN group (blue line) shows the lowest survival probability, starting around 0.95 and dropping to approximately 0.25 by 48 months. The MCL group (green line) shows intermediate survival, starting around 0.95 and dropping to approximately 0.45 by 48 months.

L1674: Figure 7: Time to First Dose Modification for AE (AdSM Safety Population). A step function graph showing the cumulative percentage of patients requiring dose modification over time (Months to first dose modification). The Y-axis ranges from 0.0 to 1.0. L1676: Kaplan-Meier curve showing Overall Survival (OS) probability over time (months) for Midostaurin (solid line) and Placebo (dashed line) arms. The Midostaurin curve is consistently higher, indicating better survival. The curve for Midostaurin plateaus around 50% survival after 36 months, while the Placebo curve plateaus around 40% survival after 36 months. The legend indicates Midostaurin (solid line) and Placebo (dashed line). Below the graph, the hazard ratio is 0.77, 95% CI (0.63, 0.96), and Log rank p value = 0.019.

L1678: Kaplan-Meier plot showing Overall Survival (OS) from the start of maintenance therapy for Midostaurin (n=41/120) and Placebo (n=32/85) groups. The Y-axis is Probability of survival (%) and the X-axis is Time (months). The Midostaurin curve is consistently higher than the Placebo curve. L1679: Figure 5 is a Kaplan-Meier plot showing Event-Free Survival (EFS) probability versus Time (months) for the Midostaurin and Placebo arms of Study A2301.
The plot displays two survival curves: Midostaurin (solid line) and Placebo (dashed line). The Midostaurin curve consistently shows a higher probability of EFS than the Placebo curve.
Statistical results are displayed on the plot:
Hazard ratio 0.804
95% [CI 0.678, 0.953]
Log rank p value = 0.012
The X-axis represents Time (months) from 0 to 84. The Y-axis represents Probability of EFS from 0.0 to 1.0.
L1680: Flowchart illustrating the Study ADE02T Trial Schema:
FLT3 Mutation Screening Within 48 Hours* (Optional: Patients may receive hydroxyurea during screening phase)
Daunorubicin Cytarabine** (Optional 2nd cycle in patients achieving PR after cycle 1)
High-Dose Cytarabine*** (Optional for patients before allogeneic HSCT)
Following High-Dose Cytarabine, patients proceed to either:
1st priority: Allogeneic HSCT, followed by 1-yr Maintenance (Start: Day 30 after allo HSCT). PKC412 is indicated below the maintenance phase.
2nd priority: 3x High-Dose Cytarabine, followed by 1-yr Maintenance. PKC412 is indicated below the maintenance phase.
Notes:
* Patients may receive hydroxyurea during screening phase
** Optional 2nd cycle in patients achieving PR after cycle 1
*** Cytarabine: 18-65 years, 3g/m2, q12hr, day 1,3,5; >65 years, 1g/m2, q12hr, day 1,3,5; optional for patients before allogeneic HSCT
